## D221 The plugins' Node subprocess finds Node beyond PATH

Summary: Evaluating chord plugins starts Node from `PIG_NODE`, from `node` on PATH, or from a version manager's or installer's usual location, whichever first is Node 22.19.0 or newer.

Audience: runtime

Stock disposition: inert capability. Stock PiG discovers a Node that is already installed. It selects no Node for the user, ships none and installs none; a Package or Piglet that ships Node is not part of Stock (deferred to PiG Standard after 0.7.0).

What: Pi evaluates plugins in the process that runs Pi, so it has no Node to find. PiG's plugins component (`chord/nextnode/plugins`) runs Pi's evaluator in a Node subprocess and finds Node in this order, running `--version` on each candidate (stopped after 10 s, so a candidate that hangs does not hold the search; the whole search stops at `NodeOptions.ReadyTimeout`) and taking the first that is `22.19.0` or newer (Pi's `engines.node`):

1. `PIG_NODE`, an executable. When it is set it is the only candidate: a value that is not an executable, does not run or is too old is the error.
2. `node` on `PATH`.
3. Unix: `$NVM_BIN`; the newest installs of `$NVM_DIR/versions/node` or `~/.nvm/versions/node`; fnm's `node-versions` (in `$FNM_DIR`, else `$XDG_DATA_HOME/fnm`, `~/.local/share/fnm`, `~/.fnm` and, on macOS, the older `~/Library/Application Support/fnm`); the newest install of Volta (`tools/image/node`) and of mise (`installs/node`); asdf's `shims`; `/usr/local/bin`; `/opt/homebrew/bin`; `/usr/bin`. Windows: `%NVM_SYMLINK%`; the versions of `%NVM_HOME%` or `%APPDATA%\nvm`; fnm's `node-versions` (`%FNM_DIR%` or `%APPDATA%\fnm`); Volta's installs; `%ProgramFiles%\nodejs`; `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\nodejs`. The shims of Volta and mise are not run: a shim downloads the Node a project pins, and the install directories hold the Nodes already there.

A candidate that is too old does not end the search. When none satisfies, the error names the requirement and the newest version found, or says that Node was not found on PATH or in the places searched. The TypeScript extension runtime slot ("TypeScript extension runtime slot" above) holds Bun and executable runtimes, not Node, so it is no source. The extension host's own Node check (`ensureNodeRuntime`) still reads PATH only.

Why: a GUI or a service starts without the shell's PATH, where version managers put `node`; users with several versions need to name one. The plan is `docs/plan/chord-plugins-node-evaluator.md` sections 6 and 10.12 (owner decision (c), 2026-11-01).

Call-site markers: `chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_node_discover.go` (`discoverNode`).

Locked by: `TestDiscoverNodeFindsNodeInEachPlaceAfterPATH`, `TestDiscoverNodeFindsNodeNamedByPIGNODE`, `TestDiscoverNodeWithAnUnusablePIGNODEIsAnErrorWhateverElseIsInstalled`, `TestDiscoverNodePrefersPATHToTheInstallLocationsAndEachPlaceToTheNext`, `TestDiscoverNodeSkipsATooOldCandidateForANewerLaterOne`, `TestDiscoverNodeWhenEveryNodeIsTooOldNamesTheNewestVersionFound`, `TestDiscoverNodeFindsNothingAndNamesTheRequirementAndWhereItLooked` and `TestDiscoverNodeTakesTheNewestInstalledVersionOfAVersionManager`, `TestDiscoverNodeFindsFnmAtItsDefaultDirectoriesWithoutFNMDIR`, `TestDiscoverNodeTakesAVersionPrintedWithoutTheVPrefix` (`chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_node_discover_test.go`), `TestDiscoverNodeStopsAProbeThatDoesNotAnswerAndTriesTheNextCandidate`, `TestStartNodeBoundsFindingNodeByTheReadyTimeout` and `TestDiscoverNodeNeverRunsVoltaOrMiseShimsAndFindsTheirInstalls` (`chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_node_discover_unix_test.go`), each on fake `node` executables in a temporary tree.

Remove when: Pi evaluates plugins in a runtime PiG does not start, or PiG Standard ships a Node and Stock drops the search.

Approval: owner decision relayed 2026-11-01: option (c), discovery beyond PATH, for difference #10.
Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 2 re-approval, the owner's dated word covers the current text; checked by Claude and Tern
SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:37962e601175fd28

## D223 The plugins' Node subprocess: a store, a spare process, exit details, and the link waits it needs

Summary: A program that evaluates chord plugins can give them a SQLite store, keep a spare Node process warm, read why the process ended, and wait for a host to handle a link's deliveries.

Audience: author

Stock disposition: inert capability. Nothing selects a store, a spare process or a wait for the user: a program that embeds `chord/nextnode/plugins` sets them, and a host that takes a link calls `Taken` only for a link that asks to be told.

What: Pi evaluates plugins in the process that runs it, so these have no Pi counterpart; each is the Go side of running Pi's evaluator in a subprocess.

- `StoreOptions` (`RuntimeLoad.Store`, `PluginsLoad.Store`): the SQLite file the Node host's store lives in, opened in the subprocess by Pi's own `next/store` and closed after the host, as Pi's `createHost({ store })` apps do.
- `NodeOptions.Warm`/`KeepWarm` (`PluginRuntime.Warm`) and `NodeOptions.StopGrace`: a spare process started ahead of a Load, so Load does not wait for Node to start; the grace (5 s, Pi's `GRACE` of `workers.ts`) is how long a process whose channel ended gets to exit before it is killed. A process running the close it was asked for (Pi ends every object at once, then drains and closes them one by one, each step bounded by `closeTimeout`) is not killed before one `closeTimeout` plus the grace has passed.
- `NodeExit.Code`, `Signal` and `Reason`: how the process ended (`process.exit(n)`, the signal, and why the Go side killed it), where Pi's in-process plugin ends the app.
- `host.DeliveryAwaiter` and `link.Channel.Idle`: a link to another process asks the host that takes it to wait until what the link handed it so far has been handled, as a host's own `Reload` waits before it returns its report; `Idle` returns when the channel is closed.

Why: the plugins' evaluator is a Node subprocess with no shared loop with the Go host, so the app needs these to persist, to start quickly, to report a crash, and to order its requests with the link's deliveries. The plan is `docs/plan/chord-plugins-node-evaluator.md` sections 4 and 10.

Call-site markers: `chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_node_store.go` (`StoreOptions`), `chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_node.go` (`NodeOptions.StopGrace`, `NodeExit`), `chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_node_warm.go` (`PluginRuntime.Warm`), `chord/next/host/host_link_deliveries.go` (`DeliveryAwaiter`), `chord/next/link/channel.go` (`Channel.Idle`).

Locked by: `TestStoreAPluginsPersistedDocumentsAndTableSurviveClosingTheNodeHost` (`chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_node_store_test.go`), `TestNodeWarmStartsOneSpareTheNextLoadTakes`, `TestNodeRuntimeClosePastItsDeadlineStillStopsTheSpareThatIsStarting`, `TestNodeCloseDoesNotKillAHostThatIsStillClosingWithinItsCloseTimeout`, `TestNodeCloseKillsAHostThatDoesNotAnswerWithinTheGrace`, `TestNodeTheDefaultStopGraceIsPisFiveSeconds` and `TestNodeAPluginsProcessExitEndsOnlyTheHostWithItsCode` (`chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_node_lifecycle_test.go`), `TestDeliveryAwaiterIsTakenOnceBeforeTheHostSendsOnTheLinkOrListensToIt` (`chord/next/host/host_link_deliveries_test.go`) and `TestIdleWaitsForTheHeldMessageAndForTheOnesBehindIt` (`chord/next/link/channel_idle_test.go`).

Remove when: Pi evaluates plugins in a runtime PiG does not start, or the program embedding the evaluator drops them.

Approval: owner decision relayed 2026-11-01 for the plugins' Node evaluator (plugfull lane), the same as D221.
Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 2 re-approval, the owner's dated word covers the current text; checked by Claude and Tern
SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:e28f85e181f840e8

## D232 Multi-session RPC server (--listen) and shared extension cells

Summary: `pig --mode rpc --listen <path>` serves one Pi RPC session per connection in one process, each with its own extension processes unless `--listen-share-extensions` shares them.

Audience: runtime

Stock disposition: inert capability. Only `--listen` selects it: without the flag no Host joins a cell registry, no process hook changes and every mode, record and wire frame is what it was. `--listen-share-extensions` is valid only with `--listen` and is an error without it.

What: `pig --mode rpc --listen <path>` listens on the Unix domain socket `<path>` and serves one Pi RPC session per accepted connection, in one process. By default every session's Extension Host spawns its own extension processes, as a process per session would, so no extension state is shared between sessions. With `--listen-share-extensions` the Hosts of the connections share the extension cell processes through a process-wide cell registry, so sessions that load the same extensions run one Go, Rust, Python or Node process per cell instead of one per session, and share that process's module-level state.

Current behavior:
- `--listen` requires `--mode rpc` and rejects `--continue`, `--resume`, `--session`, `--session-id` and `--fork` with an argument diagnostic. Every connection creates a new Session, persisted unless `--no-session` is given.
- The socket path must not exist: an existing path of any kind (a regular file, a directory, a live or stale socket, a symlink, a dangling one too) is an error PiG never clears, so a mistyped path cannot cost the user a file. On Unix PiG binds the socket inside a fresh directory of mode 0700 next to the path, sets mode 0600 and hard-links it into place, which fails instead of replacing a path that appeared meanwhile, so the socket is private from the moment the path exists and no process-wide umask is changed (a umask set around the bind would strip permission bits from files any other goroutine creates meanwhile). A path longer than a socket address holds (104 bytes on macOS, 108 on Linux) is refused. On Windows the server listens on an `AF_UNIX` socket (Windows 10 version 1803 or later); named pipes are not supported, and a socket file keeps no mode bits, so the server checks its access list before it accepts (see Who may connect). PiG removes the socket at exit only while the path still names the socket it created. The process ignores its own stdin and runs until a termination signal. A crash or `SIGKILL` leaves the socket file behind on every OS, and the next start refuses the path as existing until the operator deletes it by hand.
- Who may connect: nobody is authenticated. The socket is a Unix domain socket at the path the user gives, never a TCP port or a network interface, and the file's access is the only gate: mode 0600 on Unix, so the user's own processes (the sessions' own tools, a sandbox or container that runs as that user with the directory mounted) and root can connect and drive a full session with the server's credentials, tools and approvals. On Windows a socket file keeps no mode bits and inherits the access list of its directory, so after the bind and before the first accept the server reads the socket's list and refuses it, closing the listener and removing the socket it created, unless only the user's own SID, SYSTEM and Administrators hold data access (read, write, append, or the right to change the list or the owner). An entry for Everyone, Users, Authenticated Users, another user or a group the user belongs to is refused with its SID and well-known name; a NULL or unreadable list, a user it cannot read and an entry type it does not know are refused; access-denied entries, inherit-only entries, CREATOR OWNER and CREATOR GROUP entries and allow entries without data access are ignored (the check does not evaluate entry order, so a denied entry never excuses an allow entry). The message tells the user to serve from a directory only they can access, for example one under `%LOCALAPPDATA%`. There is no option to switch the check off. A symlink in a parent component of the path is followed, as the kernel resolves every path, so the user keeps the socket in a directory only they can write, such as `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`.
- What a connection gets: the session the user's own terminal run would have in the server's working directory, with the built-in tools (`bash` and the file tools), the extensions, the user's provider credentials through the model, and the settings. A connection has no project-trust prompt of its own: its build takes the decision the server's startup build resolved for the working directory (`--approve`, `--no-approve`, the saved decision or the settings default), so a client cannot widen it.
- Isolated (the default): every session's extension instances run in processes of that session's Host. Module-level and package-level state (a module variable, `process.on`, `process.env`, `globalThis` or a timer in Node; a package variable in Go, Rust, Python or Lisp) belongs to one session. Extensions compiled into the host program (a Piglet Binary's fused Go extensions, a program's `ExtensionFactories`) are not processes: they run inside the server process in both modes, so their package-level state is the server's and is shared by every session.
- Shared (`--listen-share-extensions`): every session's instance of an extension runs in one OS process per cell, so module-level and package-level state is shared between sessions (the examples in the first of these two bullets). Exact standalone extensions and quarantined cells keep a process of their own, so their state stays per session. Only state kept in the factory closure or in the instance is per session (see Scrutinized consequence). The flag's help line says: WARNING: module-level and package-level extension state is then shared between sessions.
- The connection's bytes are the session's stdin and stdout; the session's RPC protocol is Pi's, unchanged. A closed connection is that session's stdin end: PiG runs the session's shutdown, closes the connection and keeps serving; the exit code is logged at debug level. A closed connection also retires its own Extension Host, so its extension processes end with it, and in shared mode a shared process ends with its last generation.
- The startup build serves the first connection; every later connection builds through the cwd-bound factory a session replacement uses, concurrently in the default mode and one build at a time with `--listen-share-extensions` (a `new_session` or `switch_session` build included). Sessions run concurrently. Every session has the server's cwd, agent directory, flags, model and tool selection.
- Every connection builds its own `ModelRuntime`, as Pi's `createRuntime` does per replacement: an extension's `registerProvider` and `registerVirtualModel` land in the runtime, so a shared one would register twice and the first session to close would unregister from the other. The bench below records what the per-connection build costs.
- `SIGINT` (Ctrl+C) is a termination signal under `--listen`, like `SIGTERM` and `SIGHUP`; Pi's rpc mode leaves it to its default action.
- A termination signal runs every live session's shutdown concurrently, flushes each connection's pending records (bounded to 5 s per connection, so a peer that stopped reading cannot wedge the server), terminates the extension processes after the shutdowns and exits with `128 + signal`. A second signal abandons sessions whose dispose hangs, closes a stalled connection at once, and terminates the extension processes of the live Hosts; its listener stays live from the first signal's fan-out until the server returns, which exits with `128 + signal`.
- A `--listen` server, in both modes (the burst of a shared server is smaller, but its heap goal grows the same way, and gating the settler would add a second mode to explain for no gain), returns the heap of a burst of session starts to the operating system once no build has finished for 3 seconds (`debug.FreeOSMemory` through `heapSettler`): sessions that start together allocate their builds' garbage side by side, so the heap goal of that moment was 1.8 GB for 421 MB live at 100 sessions with two extensions, and an idle server would have kept the pages until Go's forced collection two minutes later (the server's resident memory at 100 sessions was 1.25 to 1.43 GB at turn 5, and is 0.79 GB with the settler). The extension processes show the same effect: a Node process that started seconds ago holds about 8 MB more than it will after half a minute idle (V8 trims its heap when idle), so the bench samples after an idle `--settle`.
- A failed `accept` that is transient (`EMFILE`, `ENFILE`, `ECONNABORTED`, or an error that reports `Temporary()`) is logged to stderr and retried after 5 ms, doubling up to 1 s, as Go's `net/http` `Server.Serve` does (`net/http/server.go`); only another accept error ends the server, after shutting its sessions down.
- At most 64 connections are open at once (`listenMaxConnections`, a fixed bound with no flag); a connection still waiting for the build lock counts. A connection past the bound receives one line, the response rpc mode gives an error that belongs to no command (`{"type":"response","command":"parse","success":false,"error":"too many connections (max 64)"}`, no `id`), and is closed; the line is written inline in the accept loop under a 1 s write deadline, so a peer that never reads cannot hold the loop. The server logs one stderr line when it enters the at-cap state and none while it stays there; a closed connection frees its slot. Below the bound nothing changes for a client.
- Under `--listen`, D174's GOMAXPROCS caps (1 in rpc mode, 2 in general: `procpolicy.Release` restores the runtime default width at startup) and D175's GOGC 400 start do not apply, because one process runs many sessions: sessions that start together do their server-side work on every P (with the cap of 2, 40 sessions with two extensions were ready after 5.3 s, and after 1.9 s on 16 Ps), and resident memory is the server's objective. A `GOMAXPROCS` in the environment still wins.
- Builds. A default server builds its sessions concurrently: a build only spawns the session's own extension processes (the built artifact of an extension is content-addressed, so the first session that needs it compiles it, under the publisher's per-digest lock, and every other session reuses it: `TestListenConcurrentSessionsBuildAnExtensionOnce`). A server with `--listen-share-extensions` builds one session at a time (`buildMu`), because its Hosts look up and register processes in one registry. The compiled-in registry (`internal/compiledin`) and the builder's trust cache are locked for the concurrent builds.
- With `--listen-share-extensions`, a Host takes part in the registry only when the server builds it (`Host.ShareCells`); without the flag the server creates no registry and no Host calls it. Before it spawns a cell process it claims a parked process of its own retention, then asks the registry for a live, unparked, unquarantined process under the cell hash, cwd, config root and runtime tier. Found: it admits its members as a further generation on new sockets. Not found: it spawns the process and registers it. A quarantined cell starts isolated and is never shared. A crash ends every generation in the process, each Host's supervisor handles its own members, and the first Host to respawn registers the new process for the others to join. A lone member that crashes is not quarantined per Host.
- A process a Host joined can end between the lookup and the admission, because its runner exits when its last generation ends (the Node runtime's `Generations.settle` and the Go, Rust and Python runners' `live` count all do). When the first member's admission or accept fails on a joined process that has ended, the Host spawns a fresh process, registers it, and admits again, once. Each Host's group state in a Node process (event bus, quit drain) is dropped when the group's last runtime leaves, and one `beforeExit` listener serves every group's drain. The registry deletes an entry when its process's last reference releases.
- A connection's shutdown follows D234: once it has started (stdin end, `ctx.shutdown()`, or the server's signal), a later single signal does not interrupt it. The server's signal reaches the session, finds the shutdown running, releases the hold the signal's prelude took on the Session's agent events, and the server waits for that dispose to end before it exits; a repeated signal exits the server at once with `128 +` its own number, without waiting for a dispose or for stdout. A connection's end writes no `exit.log` line (D102): the server's own ending writes the one line.
- A connection that closes during a running tool call or a `ctx.ui` dialog is that session's input end: the unwind runs `session_shutdown`, cancels the running call, and then resolves the pending dialog as cancelled, so the extension's handler may resume (`confirm` resolves `false`, never `true`) on a stale context, or may not resume before its generation ends; Pi's handler likewise never completes when its process exits first. The other sessions keep their instances, and no process, Host or connection of the closed session outlives it.
- Concurrency contracts of the server: the turns of different connections overlap, so no connection's turn waits for another's; the builds of new sessions on different connections overlap in the default (isolated) mode and run one at a time only with `--listen-share-extensions`, and each connection's records keep the order Pi's rpc mode writes them. Closing a connection during a turn is that session's input end and cancels the turn; only that session's Host, extension processes and goroutines end. A termination signal during a turn cancels every running turn, runs every session's shutdown concurrently and returns only after they ended, so each `session_shutdown` has run when the server exits; a repeated signal exits at once (D234). `TestListenSessionsRunConcurrently`, `TestListenCloseDuringATurnByDefaultEndsOnlyThatSessionsProcesses` and `TestListenSIGTERMDuringATurnCancelsItAndEndsEverySessionBeforeExit` pin them.
- `Host.Load`, the single-extension API used by the auth, validate and preview commands, registers its process but never looks up a shared one; no registry-joined Host calls it.
- The Node runtime scopes its per-process bookkeeping by the Host's group: the event bus, the virtual-model and registry write holds, the quit drain, `ctx.abort()`, the sibling-tool check that marks a timer call as a tick, and the xref transport choice. Node learns its group from the cell's admission line (the factory subscribes to the bus before `ready` arrives), and the first generation of an isolated Node launcher from `PIG_EXT_GROUP` in its environment, which `cli.mjs` reads and removes; `ready.group` stays an informational wire member that the Go, Rust and Python SDKs ignore. The shared event loop keeps `turnHold`, `turnWait`, `siblingToolPending` and decoded frames per process. A quit handler suspended in one group of a shared Node process is reported only once every group's loop drains (`otherGroupServing`).
- A stale Host (its connection closed) may still release its bus listeners (`events.off`, `xref.hello`) when it joined a registry; stock Hosts are unchanged. Exact standalone extensions are isolated by design and never share.

Scrutinized consequence: sharing a process shares everything the language keeps per process, which is why it is opt-in. With `--listen-share-extensions`, a Node module variable, `process.on` handler, `process.env` write, `globalThis` property or timer, and a Go, Rust, Python or Lisp package variable, is seen by every session's instance of the extension in that process. An extension written for Pi's one-session-per-process rpc mode that keeps a client, a cache or a `pi` or `ctx` reference in such a variable sees the next session's factory overwrite it, and its timers and sockets outlive their session while another session keeps the process. A `/reload` from one connection clears the shared factory cache and re-evaluates edited modules, so every session's next generation gets the new module version. Only state kept in the factory closure or the instance is per session; registration, replicated state, events and `session_shutdown` stay per instance. The owner decided on 2026-11-06 that the default is isolation, so a server that does not pass the flag has none of these effects, and the sharing is an informed choice. The cost of the default is memory and start-up time per session (Why). Extensions compiled into the host program run inside the server process in both modes and share their package-level state in both. `TestListenIsolatesExtensionProcessesAndModuleStateByDefault` pins the default, `TestConformance_SharedCellsShareModuleLevelState` and `TestListenSharesExtensionProcessesAcrossSessions` the opt-in.

Pi source: none. `rpc-mode.ts` serves one session per process (`main.ts:951`, `runRpcMode(runtime)`), and `createRuntime` builds a fresh runtime per replacement (`main.ts:733-775`; `agent-session-services.ts:140-147`, `ModelRuntime.create`). Pi's SDK shares one resource loader across sessions from one services object (`agent-session-services.ts:214-233`); PiG keeps one extension instance per session instead.

Why: a session's cost in one process per session is the Go runtime and relocated data segments plus every extension process (45 to 90 MB each). One server hosting the sessions removes the first part, and with `--listen-share-extensions` the second. Bench (`bench/sessionhost`, Linux, Intel Xeon Platinum 8468 host `xd6702` (container limited to 16 CPUs and 200 GB), `pss` 40 s after turn 5, five turns per session against a scripted model, 0 errors, 2 model requests per turn per session). Arms: `listen` (the default, own extension processes per session), `listen-shared` (`--listen-share-extensions`), `rpc` (one `pig --mode rpc` per session). Without extensions, 1, 10 and 100 sessions: `listen` 51.6, 67.2 and 143.6 MB, `listen-shared` 51.4, 65.1 and 136.0 MB, `rpc` 50.7, 147.8 and 1106.3 MB; slope per additional session 0.93, 0.85 and 10.66 MB. With a Go and a Node extension in every session: `listen` 166.8, 623.7 and 5252.0 MB with 2, 20 and 200 extension processes, `listen-shared` 163.4, 265.3 and 892.4 MB with 2 at every N, `rpc` 198.8, 1132.4 and 10108.2 MB with 2, 20 and 200; slope 51.4, 7.4 and 100.1 MB. The default therefore costs about half of a process per session once extensions load, and the opt-in about a tenth. Start-up, with the two extensions: the server's socket exists about 0.3 s after the start in every `listen` arm (the startup build loads the extensions), and 100 sessions are ready 4.95 s after their connections in the default, 3.69 s with the opt-in and 5.42 s for 100 `rpc` processes; without extensions 100 sessions answer within 0.21 s (default) and 0.65 s (opt-in). The default's start-up was 23.9 s before its builds ran concurrently and the cap of 2 Ps was released (README of the bench). Memory read at turn 5 rather than settled was 6538 MB for the default at 100 sessions: the extra over the serialized server's 5414 MB was transient (Node heaps that V8 trims after a few idle seconds, and the server's start-up burst of garbage, returned by `heapSettler`) and is gone after the settle (bench README).

Call-site markers (`// pig additive (D232)`): `coding/cli/args.go` (`Listen`, `ListenShareExtensions`, the `--listen` and `--listen-share-extensions` parse and diagnostics, the help lines), `coding/cli/main.go` (GOGC and GOMAXPROCS skips, `procpolicy.Release`, the build mutex only with the opt-in, the registry created only with `--listen-share-extensions`, SIGINT, dispatch), `coding/cli/rpc_listen.go` (`connectionFlushTimeout`, `flushConnectionTail`, `listenControls`, `listenHosts`, `runRPCListen`, `listenTerminationSignals`, `holdForFurtherSignals`, `acceptWithBackoff`, the accept failure, `netErrorCause`, `lstatPinned`, `removeOwnSocket`), `coding/cli/rpc_listen_cap.go` (`listenMaxConnections`, `listenRefusalWriteTimeout`, `listenAdmission`: the connection bound), `coding/cli/rpc_listen_acl.go` (`windowsACE`, `windowsDACL`, `listenSocketRefusal`: the Windows rule for who may reach the socket), `coding/cli/rpc_listen_unix.go` (`listenPrivateUnixSocket`: the private bind directory), `coding/cli/rpc_listen_windows.go` (`listenPrivateUnixSocket`: the access-list check, `readSocketDACL`), `coding/cli/rpc_mode.go` (tail flush, the connection's `rpcExit` steps, Host retirement, the connection's shutdown control, which mirrors `rpcTermination.hook`), `coding/cli/rpc_listen_unix.go` (`listenPrivateUnixSocket`, `makePrivateBindDir`), `internal/procpolicy/procpolicy.go` (`Release`), `internal/compiledin/compiledin.go` (`factoriesMu`), `coding/cli/print_mode_build.go` (build mutex, `listenBuild`), `coding/cli/cli_runtime_build.go` (`cellRegistry`, `buildMu`, the Host on the registry), `coding/cli/extensions.go` (the shared Host, `cells`), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/cell_registry.go` (`CellRegistry`, `Close`, `ShareCells`, lookup, registration and `forget`), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/host.go` (`cells`, `group`, registering the process), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/host_calls.go` (stale Host bus release), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/packed_go.go` (the admission line's group field, retained-share lookup, registering, `errJoinedProcessGone` and the retry, `onLast`), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/reload_cells.go` (quarantine start, retained-share lookup and key lock), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/node_recovery.go` (lone-member crash), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/protocol.go` (`Group`), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/runtime-node/runtime.mjs` (`xrefRealm`, `busFor`, `drainFor`, `releaseGroup`, the shared `beforeExit` ## D263 A host that cannot run generated machine code can run Code Mode on wazero's interpreter

Summary: A Go program that embeds Code Mode in a macOS app signed with the Hardened Runtime can switch the sandbox to wazero's interpreter, which needs no entitlement.

Audience: runtime

Stock disposition: inert capability. Nothing selects the interpreter by default or from a setting: the `pig` command never sets it, and only a program that embeds the `codemode` package or the built-in extension's `Options` sets it. The compiler stays the default. It is not a Piglet or PiG Standard concern, because a Piglet cannot change how the host links and signs wazero.

What: Pi runs Code Mode as QuickJS in V8 inside Node, which has no engine choice, so this has no Pi counterpart. `codemode.SandboxOptions.Interpreter` (also `InlineSandboxOptions`), `codemode.ServeOptions.Interpreter` and the built-in extension's `Options.Interpreter` select `wazero.NewRuntimeConfigInterpreter()` instead of `wazero.NewRuntimeConfigCompiler()`. The engine caches key on it, so a compiler engine and an interpreter engine of one module, cache directory and heap limit are separate, and interpreter sandboxes that differ only in `CacheDir` share one engine. The interpreter keeps no compiled code, so `CacheDir` is not opened. Results match except for deep recursion (below); otherwise only speed differs: about 50 times slower on the two scripts measured in `docs/site/docs/codemode.md` (26 ms against 1.2 s, and 152 ms against 8.4 s), with a floor of over a second per execution for the VM start. Two behaviors differ: unbounded recursion ends the execution with a sandbox error (`wasm error: stack overflow`) instead of a catchable `RangeError`, and wall-clock deadlines are as long as before while the work takes about 50 times longer.

Why: wazero's compiler writes machine code into an anonymous page, makes it executable with `mprotect` and runs it. A process signed with the Hardened Runtime and without `com.apple.security.cs.allow-unsigned-executable-memory` is killed (SIGKILL) at the first execution of that page (issue #410). `com.apple.security.cs.allow-jit` alone does not help, because wazero does not map with `MAP_JIT`. The entitlement is the better fix where the host can carry it, and `docs/site/docs/codemode.md` says so; the interpreter is for hosts that cannot. The selection is not automatic: detecting the entitlement needs a semi-private `csops` call and a wrong guess either kills the process or silently costs a factor of 50.

Call-site markers: `codemode/engine_wazero.go` (`newEngine`), `codemode/types.go` (`SandboxOptions.Interpreter`), `codemode/remote_serve.go` (`ServeOptions.Interpreter`), `coding/extension/builtin/codemode/tool.go` (`Options.Interpreter`), `coding/extension/builtin/codemode/execute.go`.

Locked by: `TestSandboxOptionInterpreterSelectsTheInterpreterRuntime`, `TestInterpreterRunsDeepRecursionDifferentlyFromTheCompiler`, `TestInterpreterAndCompilerEnginesAreNotShared`, `TestInterpreterEnginesDoNotDependOnCacheDir`, `TestInterpreterSandboxRunsScripts` (`codemode/interpreter_engine_test.go`), `TestServeOptionsInterpreterReachesTheRuntime` (`codemode/interpreter_serve_test.go`), `TestExecuteOptionInterpreterSelectsTheInterpreterRuntime` (`coding/extension/builtin/codemode/interpreter_option_test.go`).

Remove when: Pi gains an engine choice for Code Mode that PiG follows, or the program embedding Code Mode drops the option.

Approval: owner scope approval for 0.7.1 row M2 (issue #410), `handoffs/0.7.1/SCOPE-PROPOSAL.md`.
SCRUTINIZED:approved


## D273 A Go handler can read the key order of an event's objects (`Context.ObjectKeys`)

Summary: A Go extension reads the keys of an object of the event it is handling in the order Object.keys gives them in Pi; Node, Rust, Python and Lisp objects already keep that order.

Audience: author

Stock disposition: inert capability. Nothing calls it unless an extension does. The order it reads is recorded when the Go SDK decodes an event (`json.ObjectOrder`, the same record that writes a handed-back object in the order it came in), which costs a context event of 3000 messages that returns nothing 2% more time and 6% more memory (a context handler's whole event, decode to reply); a call is a lookup.

What: `Context.ObjectKeys(object map[string]any) []string` (Go SDK). For an object of the event the handler is serving it returns the keys in the order the host wrote the members, for the members the object still holds, then the members the handler added in byte order; array-index keys (`"0"`, `"1"`, ...) come first, ascending, as JavaScript enumerates them. A map the handler built, or any object of an event whose payload arrived in pieces, has its keys in byte order. Limit: a Go map records no deletions, so a handler that deletes a key and sets it again leaves the key in the place the host wrote it (and `ObjectKeys` and the reply keep it there), where Pi's JavaScript object moves a re-set key last; a key the handler sets without having deleted it keeps its place in both. Node reads `Object.keys(object)`, Rust `object.as_object()?.keys()` (serde_json with `preserve_order`), Python `list(object)` and Lisp the insertion-ordered hash table's keys, so no other SDK has a new call.

Pi source: a handler's `event` objects are JavaScript objects: `Object.keys`, `for...in` and `JSON.stringify` visit their members in insertion order (integer-like keys first), which is the order the host wrote them. A Go map has no order, so the SDK records it while it decodes the event, and each object is known by its own map.

Why: the Posthorse port printed a tool call's arguments in the model's member order (recovery-results.ts `callDetails`); a Go handler had only sorted keys (review R1).

Call-site markers: `extensions/sdk/source_order.go` (`Context.ObjectKeys`).

Locked by: `TestObjectKeysAreReadInTheOrderTheHostWroteThem`, `TestObjectKeysPutArrayIndexKeysFirst`, `TestObjectKeysDoNotLeakTheOrderOfAFreedObject`, `TestAKeptContextReadsByteOrderAfterTheReply` (`extensions/sdk/key_order_test.go`), `TestUnmarshalObjectRecordsTheMemberOrder` (`extensions/sdk/json/object_order_test.go`); `TestConformance_HandedBackEventObjectsKeepTheirMemberOrder/object_keys` (`test/extension-conformance/event_echo_order_test.go`: Go, Node, Rust, Python and Lisp, isolated and packed, and fused Go, each reporting the keys of a provider payload that no sorted read produces).

Remove when: Pi gives a Go extension an ordered object type, or the Go SDK decodes event objects into one.

Approval: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-11-07 (PortPosthorse review item R1).
SCRUTINIZED:approved

## D276 A message or entry renderer of an SDK keeps per-message state and takes the mouse events of its view (`MessageViewEvent`, `EntryViewEvent`, `MessageRenderOptions.State`)

Summary: A Go, Rust, Python or Lisp extension keeps state per displayed message or entry in its renderer and gets clicks on its view's mouse regions, so a click-expanded card works in every SDK as in Pi.

Audience: author

Stock disposition: required substrate. With no extension that registers a view renderer with a mouse region, the host sends a request without events and every renderer behaves as before. The Node runtime needs no new API: it runs Pi's own renderer, whose component the host now keeps and hands the click.

What: Pi hands a message renderer the same message object on every rebuild (`custom-message.ts` `rebuild`) and the component it returns takes the mouse (`MouseRegion`, `mouse-region.ts`), so an extension keeps a card's expansion in a `WeakMap` keyed by the message and flips it in the region's closure. An SDK renderer is a request and a response, so the pieces cross the wire. `render_message` and `render_entry` carry `key` (the host's name for the displayed message or entry, one per `extension.RenderIdentity`; the transcript creates one for each message component, as Pi's `createCustomMessage` makes a new message on each rebuild, and takes an entry's from the Session by the entry's id (`Session.EntryRenderIdentity`), as Pi renders the session manager's one object per entry on every rebuild, so an entry renderer's state survives compaction, `/tree` and the resume paint; the Session drops them with its entries when its log is replaced, and the extension is told to release the state it kept for them), `rerender` (the host built the component) and `events` (the `mouse` view events its mouse regions took, in order, since the last request). The host hit-tests the region from its description (`types`, `handled`, `render`) and answers the terminal at once; it never waits for the extension. The extension applies the events before it draws, so one request carries the click and returns the card drawn again. `render_release` (host to extension) tells the extension to drop the state of a message the transcript dropped. Go: `Extension.MessageViewEvent(customType, fn)`, `EntryViewEvent`, and `MessageRenderOptions.State`/`EntryRenderOptions.State`, a map that starts empty, is the same for every call for one displayed message (the renderer's and the event handler's), and is a fresh empty map for a call that is not for one (HTML export). Rust: `Extension::message_view_event`, `entry_view_event`, `MessageRenderOptions::state`. Python: `ext.message_view_event`, `entry_view_event`, `options.state`. Lisp: `message-view-event`, `entry-view-event`, `message-render-options-state`, `entry-render-options-state`. In-process Go renderers receive the identity in `MessageRenderOptions.Identity` and `EntryRenderOptions.Identity` (the Go form of the message object). The Node runtime keeps, per key, the first decoded message object and the component the renderer last returned: the renderer receives the same object on every rebuild, and a click goes to the component that was drawn, as in Pi.

Pi source: `packages/coding-agent/src/modes/interactive/components/custom-message.ts` (`rebuild` calls the renderer with the same `message`), `custom-entry.ts`, `packages/tui/src/components/mouse-region.ts`, `packages/tui/src/tui-alt-screen.ts` (`handleSelectionMouseEvent`: a click without a drag is dispatched through the layout after an unhandled press). Pi's extensions run in the host process, so a renderer's closure and `WeakMap` live next to the component. An extension of a separate process has neither.

Why: a port of a Pi extension whose cards expand on a click (posthorse's `context-window` and `posthorse-reminder` cards) could not match Pi: the card did nothing under PiG. The SDKs cannot offer closures, so the state is a map the SDK keeps under the host's key and the closure is an event handler.

Call-site markers: `coding/extension/renderer.go` (`RenderIdentity`), `internal/codingagent/session_render_identity.go` (`entryRenderIdentities`), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/render_keys.go` (`renderKeys`), `extensions/sdk/renderer_events.go` (`MessageViewEvent`, `EntryViewEvent`), `extensions/sdk-rs/src/extension.rs` (`message_view_event`, `entry_view_event`), `extensions/sdk-py/pig_sdk/__init__.py` (`message_view_event`, `entry_view_event`). The Lisp SDK (`extensions/sdk-lisp/src/renderer.lisp`) is not scanned by `check-divergence-consistency.sh`.

Locked by: `TestRendererClickAcrossSDKs` (`test/extension-conformance/renderer_click_test.go`: the in-process Go reference, Go, Node, Rust, Python and Lisp, isolated and packed, and fused Go; a click is handled and counted by the extension's own state with the coordinates local to the card, a press is not handled, another message has its own state, a rebuild keeps the count; message and entry), `TestRenderProxyViewMouseRegionClickReachesTheExtensionInTheNextRequest`, `TestRenderProxyRerendersOnlyWhenPiBuildsTheComponent`, `TestRenderKeysNameIdentitiesAndReleaseThemWhenCollected` (`coding/extension/host/subprocess/render_proxy_mouse_test.go`), `TestCustomMessageClickTogglesOnlyThatMessageAndIdentitySurvivesRebuilds` and `TestCustomEntryComponentHandsTheRendererOneIdentityAcrossRebuilds` (`internal/codingagent/custom_message_click_test.go`), `TestEntryRendererStateSurvivesEveryTranscriptRebuild`, `TestLiveAppendedEntryKeepsItsIdentityThroughARebuild`, `TestEntryRenderIdentitiesAreBoundedByTheEntries` and `TestEntryRenderIdentitiesGoWithTheSessionLog` (`internal/codingagent/custom_entry_identity_test.go`), each SDK's own tests (`extensions/sdk-py/tests/test_renderer_click.py`, `extensions/sdk-rs/tests/kit_wire.rs`, `extensions/sdk-lisp/tests/provider-tests.lisp`), and the tmux scenarios `extensions-runtime/96-message-click-expansion` and `extensions-runtime/97-entry-click-state-survives-rebuild` (a click expands an entry card and a rebuild of the transcript keeps it expanded, as in Pi; PiG collapsed it before the Session held the identities).

Remove when: Pi runs extensions outside its process with a renderer that can hold state and a pointer handler, or the SDKs gain closures that cross the wire.

Approval: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-11-07.
By owner principle (decision 29, 2026-11-17), checked by Claude and Tern
SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:f80c04bf30c56e66

## D614 One data-only UI protocol for plugins: mounts, intents and client abilities over D107 views

Summary: An extension in any language can mount a view in a slot the attached client draws and answer the user's intents, without the client running any plugin code.

Audience: author

Stock disposition: inert capability. No client attaches by default, so `ClientAbilities` is absent, no slot exists and every mount fails with `unsupported`. A frontend that draws mounts calls `UIBridge.SetClientAbilities`; an extension selects the protocol by calling it. Nothing in Stock PiG mounts anything. An RPC client attaches with `ui_view_attach`; until it does, RPC mode writes no carrier line. A Piglet attaches the terminal with `viewClient: terminal` (PiG Standard sets it); a run with no Piglet, or with a Piglet that does not set the key, attaches none.

What: Pi's extension UI is in-process component objects, so a Go, Python, Rust, Lisp or subprocess extension cannot reach a client other than the terminal. This record extends D107's `ui.view.*` namespace with persistent, plugin-owned views.

- Host calls `ui.view.mount`, `ui.view.update`, `ui.view.hide`, `ui.view.show`, `ui.view.finish`, `ui.view.remove` and `ui.view.state`. A mount is `{key (1..128 bytes), slot, target?, view, intents?, assets?, fallback?}`. `view` is the D107 `View`; `slot` is one of notify, status, widget-above, widget-below, header, footer, dialog, overlay, panel, tool-card, message, entry (tool-card, message and entry need a `target`). Records are closed: an unknown field, slot or node kind rejects the call with code `invalid`, and no mount changes.
- A mount is named by `{key, epoch, rev}`. `epoch` rises with every mount, `rev` with every accepted update. An update carries the ref it was computed from and is rejected with `stale` unless both are current, so an old snapshot never overwrites a newer one. The states are live, hidden, finished, removed and broken; the last three are terminal and the first one reached wins (a later operation fails with `terminal`). A key whose mount ended can be mounted again under a new epoch.
- Intents. A mount declares `IntentDecl {name "<plugin>.<verb>", nodeKey?, arg, answer?, cancellable, timeoutMs 100..300000 (default 30000)}`. `arg` and `answer` are shapes of a closed JSON subset (boolean, string, integer, number, enum of at most 32, object of at most 32 properties without extra members, array of at most 64, depth 4). A client raises an intent on a mount ref; the host refuses an unknown intent or an argument outside its shape without reaching the plugin, and otherwise sends the plugin the request `ui.view.intent {mount{key,epoch}, rev, nodeKey?, intent, arg, ticket}`. The plugin answers `{outcome: done|refused|failed, answer?, error?}`; the host adds `cancelled`, `timeout` and `stale` (an answer for a mount that changed while the plugin answered). An answer outside the declared `answer` shape is `failed`.
- `ClientAbilities {slots, nodes, markdown, imageFormats, limits {nodes, depth, snapshotBytes, textBytes, mounts, intentArgBytes}, input {keyboard, pointer, text}}` is replicated to every extension in the state snapshot (`state.uiAbilities`) and read before mounting (`ViewAbilities`). It is absent while no client is attached, and a full snapshot replaces it, so an absent value clears it. The host enforces the client's limits at mount and update. A client is sent only the node kinds its `nodes` list names: a content holding a kind outside the list does not fit that client and is sent its fallback, as for a limit, and an empty or absent `nodes` list draws no kind, as an empty `slots` list draws no slot (approved with the grid node on 2026-11-12T23:22:43Z, as quoted in the grid node's paragraph below: "no declared node kinds = none drawn").
- Amendment, owner-approved design 2026-11-10 (via Main): a mount the host ends carries why. The state is `broken` and the reason is one of `session_replaced`, `owner_gone` and `shutdown` (`ui.view.state` returns it); no fourth record exists for it. A mount ends as `session_replaced` when a new session, a fork or a switch of session ends without being cancelled (`ViewsSessionReplaced` in `coding/extension/host/subprocess/ui_view_mount.go`, called from the three session calls of `ui_bridge.go`); `owner_gone` is a reload's (see the reload rule below) and `shutdown` is the host's.
- Amendment, owner-approved design 2026-11-10 (via Main): intent cancellation is delivered through the request's ordinary cancel frame, not a message of its own. A cancellable intent whose timeout elapses, or whose caller gives up, is settled `timeout` or `cancelled` at once and its handler's request is cancelled (`ctx` cancellation, `AbortSignal`, `is_cancelled`); an answer that arrives after that is dropped, and a late answer never replaces the settled outcome. A non-cancellable intent's handler is not cancelled; its late answer is dropped. A call the handler makes after the cancel is cancelled with its request, as for every other request.
- Amendment of 2026-11-12 (the owner ordered the plugin UI port into 0.8.0; the owner approved this text on 2026-11-12T20:58Z in the words "D614 amendment APPROVED: N1 to N7 with the section 4 bounds, F4, F5", relayed through Main). It replaces the earlier list of members not taken. Every member is optional and closed: an unknown member, kind or operator rejects the call with `invalid` and changes nothing. No member carries a version, a vocabulary name or a negotiation step.
  - Claims and alternatives. `ClientAbilities.claims [{name, value}]` (at most 64; `name` is `<area>.<fact>` in at most 64 bytes; `value` is a boolean, a non-negative integer, a string of at most 256 bytes, or a list of at most 32 such strings) and `slotClaims [{slot, claims}]`, whose claims replace the same-named ones for mounts of that slot. A mount or update may carry `alternatives [{when, view, drawing?}]` (at most 8). `when` is `{any: [{all: [{claim, op, value?}]}]}` (at most 8 groups of 8 clauses) with the operators `present`, `equals`, `contains` (every listed string is in the claimed list) and `atLeast` (integers). The host sends each client the first alternative whose `when` holds for that client's claims in the mount's slot, otherwise `view`.
  - Drawing. `ClientAbilities.drawing {profiles, shapes, pixels}` and an optional `drawing` on a mount, an update and an alternative: `{profile: scene | pixels, node?, viewport? {x, y, width, height, fit: contain | cover | stretch}, shapes?, pixels? {width, height, asset, scaling: nearest | smooth}, regions?}`. A shape is a group, rect, ellipse, line, polyline (`points [{x, y}]`), path (`commands [{op: move | line | quad | cubic | close, ...}]`), text or image, with `key?`, `fill?`, `stroke? {color, width}`, `opacity?` and `transform?` (six numbers). A member of another kind rejects the drawing. Bounds: 4096 shapes, nesting 8, 1024 points or commands in one shape, 1048576 pixels, and never more shapes or pixels than the client states (`limit`). `regions [{key, shape, gestures, intent, name, role?}]` (at most 64) turn a gesture (activate, press, release, drag) inside a rect, an ellipse or a closed polyline into a declared intent. A client that does not draw the profile makes the call fail with `unsupported`. The host validates and bounds a drawing; the client rasterizes it and hit-tests its regions, so the host does no per-frame and no per-pointer work.
  - Gesture argument, added 2026-11-16 (the owner approved the rule on 2026-11-13T19:27:20Z, recorded in the owner decision log, section `2026-11-13T19:27:20Z owner: "procee[d]" on the six recommendations`, whose line reads "G-arg APPROVED: a gesture's arg carries region, x, y, dx, dy only when the intent's arg shape declares them"; relayed through Main; the text had not reached this record, and `docs/ui-view-carrier.md` called it proposed, until now). A client that raises a region's intent builds the argument from the intent's declared `arg` shape and fills only the properties that shape names: `x` and `y` (the pointer in the drawing's coordinates; for an `activate` made with the keyboard, the centre of the region), `dx` and `dy` (for `drag`, the movement since the last event of that drag, in the drawing's units; 0 for every other gesture), `region` (the region's key) and `gesture` (`activate`, `press`, `release` or `drag`; the reference web client sends it as built, and it is not in the approved line, so it is recorded here as shipped). A shape that names none of them is sent `{}`, and a property of type `integer` takes the nearest whole number. No record, member, host call or carrier message is added: the host checks the argument against the shape as it does for every intent. Only the reference web client (`examples/ui-view-web`, run with Node, not part of the pig binary) raises the gesture of a region of a drawing. The terminal view client draws no drawings and raises none: its abilities state no `drawing` (`terminalViewAbilities`, `internal/codingagent/view_client.go`, pinned by `a.Drawing != nil` in `internal/codingagent/view_client_test.go`), so a mount whose drawing it cannot draw does not fit it (`fits`, `coding/uiview/uiview.go`: it is sent the mount's fallback, or the mount fails `unsupported` when no client draws it), and the client has no code that raises the gesture of a region (the only gestures it meets are those of `bindings`, which it reports `unsupported`: `internal/codingagent/view_client_bindings.go`). A `mouse-region` node of the view kit is a different thing: it is a node of a view, and its click raises the intent declared for that node. Pinned by `TestPageDrawsASceneAndItsRegions`.
  - Finish result. `ui.view.finish {mount, result?}`; `result` is any JSON value of at most 65536 bytes. The client's snapshot and `ui.view.state` carry it while the mount is finished.
  - Diagnostics. The host call `ui.view.diagnostics {key}` returns `{notices [{code, severity: info | warning | error, message?, path?, claim?, intent?, measured?, limit?, epoch, rev?, count}]}` for the current incarnation of the key. The host records a notice when it rejects an update or a transition, refuses or ends an intent, sends a client the fallback, or when a client reports that it could not draw part of a mount (`undrawn`). A message is at most 256 bytes with control characters removed. A repeat of the same code, severity, path, claim and intent raises `count`. A mount keeps 32 notices; the oldest is dropped.
  - Accessibility and focus. Beside the view and keyed by node id, so the D107 node records stay untouched: `a11y [{node, name?, description?, role?, selected?, checked?, expanded?, busy?, disabled?, readOnly?, required?, invalid?, relations? [{kind: labelledBy | describedBy | controls | owns, target}]}]` and `focus [{node, request?: mount, restore?: previous | self}]`. The order of the focus entries is the focus order and at most one requests the focus. Each list has at most 256 entries, an entry at most 16 relations, a name at most 256 bytes and a description at most 1024. `role` is one of alert, button, checkbox, dialog, group, heading, image, link, list, listitem, menu, menuitem, progressbar, radio, region, status, switch, tab, tablist, tabpanel, textbox, toolbar, tree and treeitem. A `node` or `target` that no view of the mount has is `invalid`. A client is sent the entries of the nodes its view has.
  - Attachments. On the client side of the registry (no extension sees it), `Registry.Attach(abilities, sink)` returns an attachment with a generation and every current mount resolved for that client. `UpdateAbilities` replaces its abilities and redelivers the mounts that now resolve differently. Deliveries are full snapshots numbered 1, 2, 3 per attachment. A sink that refuses a delivery opens a gap: the client is sent nothing more until `Resync` returns every current snapshot. `Detach` ends the generation, and an intent raised under an ended generation is `stale`. With several clients attached, `state.uiAbilities` is their merge (slots, node kinds, image formats and drawing profiles: union; markdown and input: or; limits: the largest; claims: those every client states with the same value). A mount is accepted when one client that draws its slot can draw it. A client that does not draw the slot is not sent the mount, and one whose limits the content exceeds is sent the `fallback` marked `degraded`, with a notice for the plugin. `UIBridge.SetClientAbilities` remains as the one-client form.
  - Canonical digests. `uiview.Canonical` is the JSON Canonicalization Scheme of RFC 8785 and `uiview.Digest` the lower-case hexadecimal SHA-256 of that form. Every delivery carries the digest of the content resolved for that client, so a client keeps what it already drew after a resync. A content RFC 8785 cannot canonicalize (a lone surrogate, a number outside a double) has no digest. RFC 8785 is an external standard.
  - Bindings, added 2026-11-12 (demo D6 needs keys and gestures an extension declares). Approved by the owner on 2026-11-13T01:13:59Z (owner decision log, section `2026-11-13T01:13:59Z owner: "Proceed with recommendations."`), relayed by Main: "BINDINGS view member APPROVED (closed key/gesture sets, 32 per mount, reserved keys, never global)." The four parts of the approved form each have a test: closed sets, an unknown key name or gesture name is `invalid` (`TestBindingsRefused`: `unknown key`, the aliases, `unknown gesture`; `TestBindingKeyTable`); 32 per mount, the 33rd is `invalid` (`TestBindingsBounds`, and the `many` probe of `TestConformance_UIViewBindings` in every SDK); reserved keys, every row of `uiview.ReservedKeys()` is `invalid` (`escape`, `tab` and `f6` with any modifiers, and `c`, `d` and `z` with `ctrl`: `TestBindingsRefused`, and `TestViewClientKeepsOnlyReservedKeys`, which walks the table); never global, a binding fires only in the mount that holds the focus and never in a hidden one (`TestViewClientFiresABindingOnlyWhileItsMountHoldsTheFocus`, `TestViewClientBindingsFollowTheTabTheUserStandsOn`, `TestTerminalViewClientFiresBindingsFromTheFocusedMount`, `TestUIViewBindingsFireFromTheFocusedMount`). A mount or update may carry `bindings [{key | gesture, intent, arg?, name?}]` (at most 32) beside the view; exactly one of `key` and `gesture` is set. `key` is a Pi key id as `registerShortcut` and `tui.ParseKey` write it: the modifiers `shift`, `ctrl` and `alt` in any order, each at most once and followed by `+`, then one base key: `a` to `z`, `0` to `9`, `enter`, `space`, `backspace`, `delete`, `insert`, `home`, `end`, `pageUp`, `pageDown`, `up`, `down`, `left`, `right`, `f1` to `f12`, or a symbol key named by the character it produces (Pi's symbol keys; `+` is one, so `ctrl++` is `ctrl` held with it, as Pi's `parseKeyId` reads it). An id is lower case, a key has one name (no `esc`, no `return`), and `shift` goes with a letter or a named key only, because the character a shifted digit or symbol produces depends on the keyboard layout. `gesture` is one of `swipe-left`, `swipe-right`, `swipe-up` and `swipe-down`. `intent` names an intent the same content declares without a `nodeKey`; `arg` is the constant argument the client raises it with (at most 1024 bytes, checked against the intent's shape; when absent the client raises `{}`, which the shape must admit); `name` (at most 256 bytes, and a label as the Title text below defines one) is what a client shows for the binding. Several bindings may raise one intent with different arguments. `invalid`: a 33rd binding, an unknown key, modifier or gesture, a trigger bound twice (keys are compared parsed, so `ctrl+shift+p` and `shift+ctrl+p` are one key), an undeclared intent or one with a `nodeKey`, an argument outside the shape, a name that is not a label, and a reserved key. The reserved keys are one table, `uiview.ReservedKeys`, tested on the parsed key: `escape`, `tab` and `f6` with any modifiers (they leave the mount, move the focus, and move it to the next mount) and `c`, `d` and `z` while `ctrl` is held (interrupt, end of input, suspend). The table holds the keys those actions have by default, and the host refuses them for every client alike, whatever one client's user has configured: the host cannot know a client's keybindings. A client whose user can give such an action another key keeps that key from every binding when the key is pressed (the terminal client does, below). The checks are made once for a record, whichever way it reaches the registry: `DecodeMount` and `DecodeUpdate` make them on a record off the wire, `Registry.Mount` and `Registry.Update` on a record built in Go, with the same errors, and a refused typed update leaves the notice a refused wire update leaves (`TestTypedContentIsCheckedLikeARecordOffTheWire`; the typed rows of `TestConformance_UIViewBindings` on the host of every SDK, isolated, packed and fused). Client rule: a binding is never global. A key goes first to the node of the mount that holds the focus; a key that node does not take fires the binding of that key; a key no binding names is the client's own. A key binding fires only while its mount holds the focus, and a mount with a key binding can hold the focus as a whole. A gesture binding fires when the gesture is made on the mount and the node under it did not take it. The host fires nothing and reads no claim: every client is sent every binding unchanged, as a member of the content its digest covers (a client that is sent the fallback is sent none), and a constant argument above a client's `intentArgBytes` does not fit that client, as any other limit. A client that takes keyboard input for a focused mount honours key bindings, and one whose pointing surface reports swipes honours gesture bindings; a client that cannot honour binding `i` of a mount it was sent reports `{code: unsupported, severity: info, path: bindings[i]}` (`Attachment.Report`, `ui_view_report`), which the extension reads with `ui.view.diagnostics`. A client may state the claim `input.bindings` (a list of `key` and `gesture`) so that `alternatives` can test it; that is a convention between clients and extensions, which the host neither reads nor checks. The claim says which kinds of trigger a client honours on a mount that holds the focus; it says nothing about one key, one slot or the user's keybindings, and a client that does not state it may still honour bindings, so the `unsupported` notices are what tells an extension that a binding was not honoured. Limits, which are the contract and not defects: a binding is never global, so while a node of the view that takes every key (an `input` or an `editor` of the view kit) holds the keys, no binding of that view fires; and a reserved key cannot be bound in any client, also where the user gave its action another key. Global keys stay `registerShortcut`.
  - Bindings in the terminal client, added 2026-11-12 (the terminal half of the bindings above, under the same approval: the owner's line of 2026-11-13T01:13:59Z quoted there; what this text adds to the approved form, the hint row and the points marked open, is the terminal client's and is put to the owner through Main). Where a key reaches a binding: a mount's key bindings are in force only while that mount holds the keyboard focus and only for a key the focused node of its view declined (`viewClient.unhandledKey`, the one place a binding fires). No binding is global: while the editor, an overlay, another mount or the tab row holds the focus, the key is theirs. A mount of `panel` that binds at least one key is focusable as a whole, with no `focus` record and no node that takes keys: F6 stops on it, and the mount itself holds the focus, not its tab entry, so a key has one path to the bindings. A tab without a key binding and without a node that takes keys still leaves the focus on the tab row, where no binding is in force, and a hidden entry has none in force: Enter on it opens it whatever it binds. The focused node has the key first: a list keeps its arrows and Enter, and an `input` or `editor` node of the view kit reports every key as taken, so while one holds the keys of a view no binding of that view fires (the kit's answer; leaving the node, or a view without one, is how its bindings are reached). That is `never global` at work: the focused node comes first. Firing raises the binding's `intent` with no `nodeKey` and its `arg`, or `{}`. One intent of a mount is in flight: a binding fired before it settled is dropped and counted, like any activation. The handler runs off the input loop: `viewClient.raise` starts the intent in its own goroutine and its result comes back to the loop as a queued task, so a handler that never answers holds no key (`TestViewClientAHandlerThatNeverAnswersHoldsNoKey`). An intent a key is bound to is no longer reported as one the terminal offers no control for. Matching: the key the terminal reported is compared with the binding's parsed key (`tui.MatchesKeyID`), never with its text, so `ctrl+shift+q` and `shift+ctrl+q` are one key. A symbol is matched by the character produced, which the terminal gives as text (the legacy encoding, and the kitty keyboard protocol as PiG enables it) or as the shifted alternate of an escape code (`CSI 47:63;2u` is `?`; `CSI 223:63;2u`, shift and `ß` on a German layout, is `?` too). A legacy `A` fires `shift+a`. Not determinable from the terminal's report, and not guessed: an escape code that names the base key and shift without the alternate (`CSI 47;2u`) fires neither `/` nor `?`, because the character depends on the layout; a legacy `A` made with Caps Lock is not told from shift and fires `shift+a`; a digit made with shift (AZERTY) fires as that digit only when it arrives as text. `+` is a key like any symbol: the text `+` and the shifted alternate `+` of an escape code (`CSI 61:43;2u`) fire it, and `ctrl++` is `ctrl` held with it. Not honoured, reported once each for a mount as `{code: unsupported, severity: info, path: bindings[i]}` when the snapshot is applied, never when a frame is drawn: every gesture (the terminal reports none) and a key of a mount in `widget-above` or `widget-below` (such a mount never holds the focus). Hint row: while the mount holds the focus and has key bindings with a `name`, one row under its view, inside the mount's rows, lists them as `key name` separated by two spaces, in the theme's `muted` colour, cut by cells with `...`; a name is a label, so the row writes it as the host accepted it, without the spaces around it. The row counts against the slot's row cap, so a view as tall as the slot is cut one row earlier. A mount without a named key binding draws byte for byte what it drew before. The client's own keys: F6, the key that interrupts (Escape by default) and Tab are rows of `uiview.ReservedKeys()`; Enter on a tab entry is kept only while the tab row holds the focus, where no binding is in force, so it needs no row. Reserved actions: the rows `escape`, `ctrl+c`, `ctrl+d` and `ctrl+z` of the table are the default keys of four actions of Pi's keybindings (`core/keybindings.ts`): `app.interrupt`, `app.clear`, `app.exit` and `app.suspend` (`viewReservedActions`; `TestViewClientReservedActionsAreTheRowsOfTheReservedTable` holds the list and the table together). The user's keybindings file (`keybindings.json` in the agent directory) can give an action other keys, and `/reload` reads the file again, so the terminal asks the keybindings when a key is pressed, not when a view is mounted: a key they give to one of the four actions reaches no binding, whatever the mount binds and whenever the keybindings changed. The key of `app.interrupt` leaves the mount, after raising a dialog's `dismiss` intent once when its mount declares one, as Escape does by default; the keys of the other three raise nothing while a mount holds the focus, as their defaults do. The plugin reads one `unsupported` notice with the path `bindings[i]` for such a binding, the first time its key is pressed. The default keys stay `invalid` when a view is mounted after the user moved their action, because the host's check is the same for every client. The hint row is written when the snapshot arrives, so it can still name such a key (`TestViewClientReservedActionsKeepTheUsersKeys`, `TestTerminalViewClientGivesNoBindingAKeyOfAReservedAction`). The claim `input.bindings` is not stated by the terminal: the contract gives it no value, and the terminal honours keys in one slot and no gesture. The reference web client's half is not part of this text.
  - Dismiss intent, added 2026-11-13 (a dialog or an overlay is closed by the user with the client's own gesture, and only the plugin can close its mount). Approved by the owner on 2026-11-13T16:41:13Z (owner decision log, section `2026-11-13T16:41:13Z owner: hero take choices`, whose decision line names the member, `IntentDecl.dismiss`; the owner's words there: "we should approve it. Proceed we need to document it as well."). `IntentDecl.dismiss: true` names the intent a client raises with `{}` when the user dismisses a dialog or overlay mount. At most one intent of a mount has the member, it names no `nodeKey`, and its `arg` shape accepts `{}`, which is what a client sends: a second one, a `nodeKey`, an `arg` that refuses `{}` and a value that is not a boolean are each `invalid`. The plugin's handler closes the mount (`ui.view.remove`, `ui.view.finish` or `ui.view.hide`). The host closes nothing by itself and no client runs plugin code: a client keeps drawing the mount until the snapshot that ends it arrives. A client raises the intent only for a dialog or overlay mount; in another slot the member has no effect. It is not `invalid` there: the check of a content does not read the slot, and one list of intents serves a view that is mounted as a panel and as a dialog. The member is independent of `whenHidden`, and one intent may have both. A client that lacks the member keeps the earlier rule: Escape leaves the mount, which stays open until the plugin's own key or control closes it. Such a client reads the snapshot as before, because the member is one optional boolean on an intent. Every SDK sends the member only when it is true (Go `ViewIntent.Dismiss`, Rust and Python `dismiss`, Lisp `:dismiss`, Node `dismiss`) and none checks it. Nothing else is added: no message, operation, claim, version or negotiation; the member rides in the `intents` of `ui.view.mount` and `ui.view.update`. This text adds the member to the host and the five SDKs; the terminal client and the reference web client raise it with their dialog work, and keep the earlier rule until then. Locked by `TestDismissIntentRules` and `TestDismissIntentIsSentToAClientAndRaisedWithTheEmptyObject` (`coding/uiview`) and `TestConformance_UIViewDismiss` (every SDK and realization).
- Amendment of 2026-11-12, titles, a view the user opens and focus on an update (rulings between Main and the maintainer on 2026-11-12 for the owner's D6 bar: views beside the chat that the user opens without asking the agent and that an agent's tool can show; the owner approved the members on 2026-11-12T23:22:43Z in the words "proceed with recommendations", recorded for this item as "New view contract members APPROVED: grid node, mount title, whenHidden intent, focus request: update + 'unfocused' notice, two live terminal panels as tabs, request: mount does not take focus from an editor holding text, no declared node kinds = none drawn.", relayed through Main). Each member is optional and closed like the rest: an unknown member or value is `invalid`, and one past a bound is `invalid`.
  - Title. `title` on a mount and on an update: a string of at most 64 bytes that is a label. A label is text a client writes on one line among its own: a mount's `title`, a mount's `key`, which labels a mount that has no title, and a binding's `name`. A label holds no C0 or C1 control character (ESC and the line breaks are among them), neither U+2028 nor U+2029, and, since 2026-11-13, none of the controls that reorder or hide the text around them: the embeddings and overrides U+202A to U+202E and the isolates U+2066 to U+2069. With one of those a label could pass for another: a tab that reads as another extension's, a key hint that names another action. The direction marks U+200E, U+200F and U+061C are accepted: Arabic and Hebrew text beside Latin holds them, and they reorder nothing on their own. The host makes the check (`offLabel` in `coding/uiview`) and answers `invalid`; no SDK validates a title, a key or a name on its side (`TestLabelsHoldNoControlThatBreaksOrReordersALine`). An empty title is no title. It is content: an update replaces it with the rest, an update without it clears it, and it is in the snapshot a client is sent and in that snapshot's digest. A client labels the mount with it, in a tab or in a list of views, and with the mount's `key` when it has none; a client with less room cuts it by display cells, never by bytes.
  - An intent a hidden mount takes. `IntentDecl.whenHidden: true`. A mount that is not live takes no intent, with this one exception: a hidden mount takes the intent it declares `whenHidden`. A finished, removed or broken mount still takes none, and a reference to another epoch or revision is still `stale`. At most one intent of a mount has the member, it names no `nodeKey`, and its `arg` shape accepts `{}`, which is what a client sends. A client offers a hidden mount to the user only when the mount declares that intent; choosing it raises the intent, and the plugin's handler calls `ui.view.show`, after an update if it wants one. No client runs plugin code or invents content: what appears is the plugin's own snapshot, and the plugin learns of the user's act by the intent itself. While the mount is live the intent is one like any other. Bounds: this record holds no rate bound for intents. The `whenHidden` intent is under the bounds of every intent: the client's `limits.intentArgBytes` (refused past it), its `timeoutMs`, and, in the terminal client, one intent in flight per mount. A client other than the terminal one can raise it as often as any other intent.
  - Focus on an update. `focus [{node, request?: mount | update, restore?}]`. `update` asks for the focus when a client first draws the revision that carries it; on a mount's first revision it means what `mount` means. At most one entry requests, as before. A request is a request: the client decides. When it does not give the focus it reports the notice `unfocused` (severity `info`, path `focus`, the reason as the message), which the plugin reads through `ui.view.diagnostics`; a repeat raises the notice's `count`. `Attachment.Report` and the carrier's `ui_view_report` take the codes `undrawn`, `unsupported`, `limit` and `unfocused`.
  - A view an agent shows needs no host tool, so Stock PiG gains none: an extension registers its own tool, and the tool's handler mounts, shows and updates with a focus request from the context it is given. `TestConformance_UIViewToolShowsAndFocuses` pins that path and the three members above in every SDK and realization: a tool registered by the fixture and called through the tool-execution path mounts a titled panel, hides it, shows it and updates it with `request: update`, the test's client must be sent exactly those snapshots, and the hidden mount then refuses its plain intent and answers `done` to its `whenHidden` one.
  - The reference web client (`examples/ui-view-web`) uses the members: it labels a mount with its `title` (its tab, its card and a dialog's accessible name) and with `<plugin> / <key>` or the key without one; it moves the focus for `request: mount` on a new mount and for `request: update` on an update, and for a dialog mount when it is shown, and reports `unfocused` instead when a text field outside the mount holds text; it draws the `grid` node. It keeps a hidden mount in the page without drawing it, and offers the user a hidden mount that declares the `whenHidden` intent: a button with the mount's title in a list above the prompt, which raises that intent with `{}`; the plugin's handler shows the view, and the page shows nothing by itself. A hidden mount without the intent is not listed, and on a live mount the intent has no button (`TestPageOffersAHiddenMountThatDeclaresWhenHidden`).
- Wire carrier, added 2026-11-12 (the owner ordered a carrier for clients outside the process into 0.8.0; the owner approved this text on 2026-11-12T20:58Z in the words "D614 amendment APPROVED: N1 to N7 with the section 4 bounds, F4, F5", relayed through Main). RPC mode, on stdin and stdout and on a `--listen` connection, carries the attachment API as JSON lines. The carrier adds no record and no member the Go API lacks. Commands: `ui_view_attach {abilities}` and `ui_view_resync {attachment}` reply `{attachment, seq, snapshots}`; `ui_view_abilities {attachment, abilities}`, `ui_view_detach {attachment}` and `ui_view_report {attachment, plugin, mount {key, epoch}, notice {code, severity, message?, path?, claim?, measured?, limit?}}` reply `{}`; `ui_view_intent {attachment, plugin, mount {key, epoch, rev}, nodeKey?, intent, arg}` replies `{ticket, outcome, answer?, error?}` once the intent settled; `ui_view_intent_cancel` carries the `id` of the intent request, is answered first and ends that intent `cancelled` (an intent sent without an `id` cannot be cancelled). Events: `ui_view {attachment, seq, snapshot}` is one delivery; `ui_view_gap {attachment, seq}` says the host refused delivery `seq`, and after it the host writes nothing for the attachment until `ui_view_resync`. These seven names are commands for every client, attached or not. Any other `type` keeps Pi's unknown-command response byte for byte, and a client that sends none of the seven is written no carrier line. A failed carrier command is Pi's error response with one more member, `code` (invalid, unsupported, limit, stale, not_found, terminal), and its `error` is `<code>: <text>`; no other response gains a member. An unknown member of a command or of a record inside it is `invalid`. Bounds: one attachment per connection at a time; at most 8 attachments per registry, counting Go API, in-process and wire clients together (the ninth attach fails `limit`); a queue per attachment of at most 256 events and 4194304 bytes, counted until the client was written them; 4194304 bytes per `ui_view` line and per carrier command line. A snapshot whose line would be larger is carried degraded, without its content, in an event and inside a reply alike, and the plugin reads a `limit` notice; a reply is therefore bounded by the client's `limits.mounts` times that bound. Assets ride inline: a mount with a `managed` asset is carried degraded and the plugin reads an `unsupported` notice. An attach, abilities or resync reply is written before any event numbered after it, and a resync supersedes the events still queued. The sink only encodes and enqueues; one goroutine per attachment waits on the client, so the registry lock, the session loop and the TUI loop never do. Closing the connection, or replacing the session, ends the attachment: intents in flight settle `stale`, and the same connection may attach again. The client page is `docs/ui-view-carrier.md`; `examples/ui-view-web` is a reference client whose bridge program is not part of pig.
- Chord surfaces as a user of the registry, added 2026-11-12 (approved with the carrier text on 2026-11-12T20:58Z, as quoted above). `internal/chordview` mounts one panel per client surface of a chord app (web, tui) under the plugin name `chord`, keyed by the surface's name: a text node with the surface's name and the hash of its base bundle, replaced when the base changes, mounted again when the host ended it, and finished when the app closes. It declares no intent. The name `chord` is the host's: every `ui.view.*` call of an extension of that name fails `invalid`. Chord imports neither `coding/uiview` nor the adapter (`make gui-import-guard`). Nothing in Stock PiG starts a chord app, so nothing is mounted.
- Amendment of 2026-11-12, redraw inside the answer and the abilities event (two findings of the demo lane, F4 and F5; owner decision of 2026-11-12T20:58:41Z, "Proceed with recommendations", recorded in the owner decision log as "D614 amendment APPROVED: N1-N7 with section 4 bounds, F4, F5 (one abilities-changed registration per SDK); all optional, closed, unversioned, inert in Stock."). No record, member, host call or carrier message is added.
  - Redraw inside the answer. An update that the answering handler makes to the mount its intent was raised on, and a mount of that key again after it ended, belongs to the answer and does not make it `stale`. The host knows such a call by the request it is made in: every SDK already names that request on a host call, and the host sent the intent as that request.
