A change by any other hand (the plugin's background work, the handler of another intent, a call made after the intent settled) makes the answer `stale` as before; hide, show, finish, remove and a break change no ref and make no answer stale. Of two intents in flight on one mount, the one whose handler updates first is answered. The carrier writes the `ui_view` events queued before an intent settled before that intent's reply.
  - Abilities event. Every SDK has one registration (`pi.onViewAbilities`, `OnViewAbilities`, `on_view_abilities`, `on_view_abilities`, `on-view-abilities`) whose handler runs when `state.uiAbilities` changes: the first client attached, the abilities changed, the last client left. The value is the new abilities; it is absent (nil, None, null, NIL) for no client, and a client that draws no slot is a record with empty `slots`. The SDK raises the event from the state snapshot it already receives, after its replica took the value; the host sends no frame for it and never waits for the handler. With several clients attached the value is their merge, as `ViewAbilities` returns it, for clients attached in process and over the carrier alike, and the handler runs when the merge changes and not when a client attaches or leaves without changing it. The state an extension starts in is not a change, and a session no client attaches to runs no handler. `Registry.OnAbilities` takes more than one function, and the host's chord panels (`internal/chordview`, `Views.Follow`) are mounted when a client arrives.
  - Locked by: `TestConformance_UIViewAnswerRedraw` and `TestConformance_UIViewAbilitiesEvent` (every SDK, isolated and packed), the tests of `coding/uiview/answer_test.go`, `TestCarrierWritesTheHandlersUpdateBeforeTheIntentReply`, `TestRPCUIViewHandlersRedrawIsWrittenBeforeTheIntentReply`, `TestRPCUIViewAbilitiesEventMountsForAClientThatAttachesToAnIdleSession`, `TestRPCUIViewBlockedAbilitiesHandlerDelaysNothing`, `TestFollowMountsThePanelsWhenAClientArrives`.
- Terminal client, added 2026-11-12 (owner decision of 2026-11-12, D1 Option A: the TUI as an in-process view client, on in PiG Standard, inert until an extension mounts). Approved by the owner on 2026-11-12T20:58Z, relayed by Main: "D614 amendment APPROVED: N1 to N7 with the section 4 bounds, F4, F5. Stamp the extension port, carrier, view-API fixes and TUI view client on their proofs." The terminal client is not one of that amendment's N1 to N7 (in `D614-AMENDMENT-FOR-APPROVAL.md` N1 is the client claims and alternatives); it is N1 of the list under the owner's D1 Option A of 2026-11-12 (the TUI as an in-process view client), and the sentence above stamps it. The Piglet key `viewClient` takes `none` (the default, Stock PiG) or `terminal`; a child Piglet inherits it and replaces it by naming it. With `terminal`, an interactive session attaches its TUI to the view registry of the bound extension host through `Registry.Attach` when the host is bound and detaches it when the host is unbound, so the attachment follows a reload and a session replacement. Print, JSON and RPC mode attach nothing for the key. Abilities: the slots `widget-above` and `widget-below` (the widget containers above and below the editor, after the widgets), `panel` (the rows directly above the editor) and `dialog` (a titled frame centred over the screen); a panel and a dialog take the keyboard focus, a widget slot never does; every node kind of the D107 vocabulary except `image`; markdown; no image format, no drawing profile and no a11y; the limits 512 nodes, depth 16, 262144 snapshot bytes, 65536 text bytes, 16 mounts and 4096 intent argument bytes; keyboard and text input, and the pointer in fullscreen mode only; the claims `client.kind` = `terminal` and `size.columns`, for each slot `size.rows`, and for `dialog` its own `size.columns`, the columns inside the frame. The abilities record has no size member, so the size is a claim; the client states it again after a resize and after a change of TUI mode. Drawing: a mount is drawn by the D107 view kit, the code path of a D107 widget's view. A widget slot draws at most 10 rows, a panel at most half the terminal's rows and never fewer than 3, and a dialog the rows inside its frame: the frame is eight tenths of the terminal's columns and eight tenths of its rows, rounded down, and its border takes one cell on every side, so a terminal of 120 columns and 40 rows gives a dialog 94 columns and 30 rows; a taller view is cut, its last row reads `... (view truncated)`, and the plugin reads a `limit` notice. A view the D107 validator rejects draws nothing and leaves an `undrawn` notice. A mount with a drawing arrives as its fallback, by the registry's rule. Focus: a panel takes the focus from the editor when a focus record of its mount has `request: mount`, on F6, or on a click. F6 is bound only while a focusable panel is drawn and moves the focus from the editor through the panels and back. It is not a keybinding id, because Pi's keybinding table has no row for it and PiG has no additive keybinding id; a terminal that does not send F6 uses the focus record or, in fullscreen mode, a click. The key bound to `app.interrupt` (Escape) returns the focus to the editor and cancels a cancellable intent of the panel in flight. It interrupts the agent only while the editor holds the focus, so two presses always reach the interrupt from a panel and from a dialog whose mount declares no `dismiss` intent, and at most three from a dialog whose mount declares one, whatever the plugin does. Every other key goes to the focused node of the view (its `focus` member, or the node of the first focus record); a key that node does not take is dropped. Intents: selecting an item of a select-list, changing a setting of a settings-list, submitting an input or an editor, and clicking a mouse-region raise the first intent declared for that node (`nodeKey`) whose argument the node fills. An object's `value` property takes the node's value and its `item` property the setting's id; a boolean, string, integer, number or enum shape takes the value itself. An intent without a `nodeKey`, or one the node cannot fill, is not offered, and the plugin reads one `unsupported` notice. The intent runs off the interactive loop and its result returns through it. One intent per mount is in flight; an activation made meanwhile is dropped and counted, because it was made on a view whose answer the user had not seen, and the registry's revision rule would settle it `stale` once the handler updates the view. A refused, failed or timed-out intent writes one warning line; a done, stale or cancelled one writes nothing; no key is replayed. Order: deliveries and intent results share one queue of at most 64 deliveries, which the interactive loop drains in order, so a handler's update is drawn before its intent's result. A full queue refuses the delivery, which is the registry's gap, and the next drain resyncs. Cost: selected and never sent a mount, the client adds no component, input listener, goroutine, timer or per-frame work, and the terminal is written the same bytes. The host's state push worker ends when it is idle, so replicating the abilities leaves no goroutine either.
  - Dialog in the terminal client, added 2026-11-13. The owner approved the dialog slot's record words of rounds 1 and 2 and the `dismiss` member on 2026-11-13T16:41:13Z, and the Escape bound on 2026-11-13T19:27:20Z (owner decisions of 0.8.0). The sentences on a dialog that does not hold the keyboard were set after that under the owner's delegation (decision entry of 2026-11-13T16:55:59Z). The sentences on what the frame covers, on the label of the row above the editor and on a `dismiss` intent in a slot other than `dialog` were set under the owner's delegation as well, and two sentences of round 1 stay out (decision entry of 2026-11-13T21:10:56Z). A live mount of `dialog` is drawn through the overlay that Pi's `ctx.ui.custom` uses (`tui.ShowOverlay`), in PiG's titled frame with the mount's `title`, or its key when it has none, as its title, over the transcript, the panels and the editor: every row of the screen that the frame covers is blank outside the frame across the whole width, the rows above and below it are drawn as they were, and what the frame covers keeps its state and is drawn again when the frame closes; a mount whose title changed is drawn in a new frame. It takes the keyboard focus when it is shown, with or without a focus record, by the rule of a focus request: from a view of the client, from the tab row and from an editor that holds no text. From an editor that holds text, or from another component, it takes none: it has no frame, the plugin reads `unfocused`, and F6 enters it. It has every key while it holds the focus: a key goes to its focused node, and one that node declines to its key bindings, as in a panel, and the hint row of its named bindings is the last of its rows inside the frame. Only the plugin closes it: a mount that is hidden or ended closes the frame and the focus returns to what held it before, and a mount shown again is entered again by the same rule. Several live dialogs are stacked in the order they were shown: the last is on top with the keyboard, and when it closes the one under it has the keyboard again. The key bound to `app.interrupt` cancels a cancellable intent of the dialog in flight and returns the focus to the editor. A frame is drawn only while a dialog holds the keyboard, so no frame covers what the user types: while none does, the dialogs are one row directly above the editor, the title of the top one, or its key when it has none, and `: F6 to enter`, and F6 draws every dialog of the stack in its frame again, the last on top with the keyboard. While a dialog is live F6 moves between the editor and the dialogs and passes the panels and the tab row by, and F6 is bound while a focusable panel or a dialog is live. The terminal offers a hidden dialog nowhere (the tab row lists panels), so the plugin reads `unsupported` for a dialog's `whenHidden` intent: a dialog is opened from a key or a node of another mount. At a reload the registry ends the mount (the reload rule below), so the frame closes, and the mount the new instance makes is entered again. In a dialog whose mount declares a `dismiss` intent, the first press of the key bound to `app.interrupt` raises that intent with `{}` and no `nodeKey` instead of leaving the dialog: the key is not given to the focused node, the dialog keeps its frame and the keyboard, and it closes when the plugin hides or ends its mount, so what closing means is the plugin's. The key asks once: pressed again while the dialog still holds the keyboard, whether the plugin answered and left the dialog open or has not answered, or pressed while another intent of the dialog is in flight (a cancellable one is cancelled), it leaves the dialog as it leaves one without a `dismiss` intent; a dialog entered again is asked again. The key offers the intent, so the plugin reads no `unsupported` notice for it; in a slot other than `dialog` the terminal does not act on the member: the key leaves the mount as it does without it, and a `dismiss` intent that nothing else of the mount offers is reported to the plugin once as `unsupported`. Locked by: `TestViewDialogCells`, the tests of `view_client_dialog_test.go` and `view_client_dialog_mode_test.go`, and in a real session under tmux `TestUIViewDialogOpensAndClosesAtBoundKeys`, `TestUIViewDialogKeysAndStack`, `TestUIViewDialogEndsAtAReloadAndOpensAgain`, `TestUIViewDialogLeavesATypingUserTheKeys`, `TestUIViewDialogEscapeDismissesOrLeaves` and `TestUIViewDialogEscapeReachesTheEditorInABoundedCount`.
- Terminal client, tabs and the focus rule, added 2026-11-12 (rulings between Main and the maintainer on 2026-11-12: panes are tabs in the terminal; an agent's tool may always show a view and may take the keyboard only from an empty editor; no new limit. The owner approved it, with the two changes of existing behaviour named at its end, on 2026-11-12T23:22:43Z, in the words quoted in the amendment of the titles above). The mounts of the `panel` slot are tabs: the column `tabs` of the client's slot table says so, a slot without it draws every live mount one under the other as before, and that column is the one decision point between the two. One tab row above the panel lists, in mount order, every live panel and every hidden panel that declares a `whenHidden` intent, each by its `title`, or by its `key` when it has none. Only the view of one tab, the front one, is drawn. The row is drawn when it lists two entries, a hidden entry or a title: one live panel without a title is drawn byte for byte as before, and a session that never mounted is the session it was. Drawing uses the theme as it is and adds nothing to it: the front tab is bold and underlined, the other live tabs are in the `muted` role, a hidden entry is in the `dim` role and in parentheses, the entry the keyboard is on stands in square brackets, and `*` in the `warning` role precedes a tab that asked to be shown while the user worked elsewhere. A label takes at most 24 cells, fewer when the row is wider than the terminal and never fewer than 4, and is cut by display cells with `...`, never inside a wide character or a combining or emoji sequence. F6 walks from the editor to the first entry, from an entry to the next, and from the last back to the editor. Arriving on a live entry makes it the front tab and gives its view the focus when the view takes keys; on an entry whose view takes none, and on a hidden entry, the tab row holds the focus. The walk never opens a hidden entry: the key that confirms (`tui.select.confirm`, Enter) on it raises its `whenHidden` intent with `{}`, once while that intent is in flight, and when the handler's `show` arrives the tab is the front one and its view has the focus. Escape returns the focus to the editor, and the tab last visited stays drawn. There is no slash command and no selector for the list of views: both would be product UI, and the tab row is the product-neutral place. Showing: a panel that turns live (mounted, or shown after a hide) or that carries a focus request becomes the front tab when the editor holds the focus or the user stands on its tab. While the user works elsewhere (in another panel, on another tab, in an overlay) the drawn view stays and the tab is marked, once, until the user visits it. Focus: a request (`mount` or `update`) is granted when the view already holds the focus (the requested node then takes the keys), when the user stands on its tab, or when the editor holds the focus and holds no text. Otherwise the client reports `unfocused` with the reason: `the editor holds text`, `another component holds the keyboard focus`, or `the terminal gives the nodes of the view no keyboard focus`. A client cannot tell an update a tool made from any other, so the rule covers every request. Not built: a click on a tab label in fullscreen mode. Changes of existing behaviour, approved with the members: (i) before, every live panel was drawn, one under the other, and F6 went from one focusable panel to the next; after, panels are tabs as above. (ii) before, `request: mount` took the focus whenever the editor held it; after, only when the editor also holds no text. Two existing tests changed and none was deleted: `TestViewClientFocusKeyCyclesPanels` keeps its body and now runs with the panel slot's `tabs` off, where it pins the walk as it was (for (i)); `TestMountSurfaceDefaultFocusAndRejectedView` expected a view equal to the one drawn to stay without a focused node when the client named one, and now expects that node to take the keys while the view is still not built again (a focus request on an update that changes nothing else must move the keys). Locked by: `TestViewClientPanelsAreTabs`, `TestViewClientFocusKeyWalksTheTabs`, `TestViewClientCutsATabLabelByCells`, `TestViewClientStacksThePanelsOfASlotWithoutTabs`, `TestTerminalViewClientDrawsPanelsAsTabs`, `TestTerminalViewClientOneUntitledPanelDrawsAsWithoutTabs`, `TestViewClientGrantsAFocusRequestOnlyFromAnEmptyEditor` and, in a real session under tmux, `TestUIViewPanelsAreTabs`.
- Terminal client, image node. The bytes of an image are not text, and the terminal's snapshot bound stays. Where the terminal has no image protocol the client draws the node as half-block cells (`coding/extension/host/subprocess/view_half_block.go`). The terminal client declares the node kind `image` and the image formats `png`, `jpeg`, `gif` and `webp`. The registry's longest-text figure, which a client's `limits.textBytes` bounds, leaves out the `data` member of the records of a view's own `images` list: the bytes of an image are not text. They count in `limits.snapshotBytes` as the base64 they are carried in, so the terminal's 262,144 holds an image of about 195,000 bytes and a larger one is refused with `limit`. Before, an image above 49,152 bytes was refused as text by every client that takes 65,536 bytes of text. A mount surface draws the node with a component of its own (`coding/extension/host/subprocess/view_mount_image.go`), not with the chat image component: the bytes are measured and, for the Kitty protocol, converted to PNG off the loop, once per image for all mounts of the client, and the node draws one empty row until then. Its width is the smaller of the mount's width less 2 and `maxWidthCells` (60 when absent); its height is the smaller of `maxHeightCells` (when absent, the height of that width as a square) and the rows of the slot; the aspect is kept. The rows of the view's other nodes are not subtracted. In kitty and Ghostty, named by their environment where the TUI detected the Kitty protocol, the image is transmitted once with a virtual placement and drawn as Unicode placeholder cells: an update that keeps the image, a second mount of it and the end of one of two mounts send no bytes again, a change of its cells transmits it for the new cells, and the end of the last mount frees it. Another terminal with the Kitty protocol and a terminal with the iTerm2 protocol are sent the rows of the chat image component, built from the prepared bytes, with one image id per image. A terminal without an image protocol, tmux included, draws the image as half-block cells (U+2580, the upper pixel as the foreground colour and the lower as the background, in 24-bit colour or the 256-colour palette as the terminal has), drawn off the loop for the node's box of cells; bytes that cannot be decoded, or an image of more than 40,000,000 pixels, draw that component's one line of text. The image is not sent again after a full redraw: kitty 0.50.0 keeps an image that has only a virtual placement when the TUI deletes all images (`a=d,d=A`, `tui/tui_alt_screen.go` `deleteKittyImages`), clears the screen, or leaves and enters the alternate screen (run on kitty 0.50.0 only, in a real window on 2026-11-13; Ghostty was not run). In iTerm2 the image is drawn only outside the alternate screen, as Pi's images are (`packages/tui/src/tui-alt-screen.ts` `beforeTerminalStart`; `tui/tui_alt_screen.go`); in fullscreen mode the node is the one line of text. Every SDK mounts an image node in a conformance row, isolated and packed (`TestConformance_UIViewImageID`, which pins the Kitty image id the author sets); the dialog slot is described in its own bullet below. Locked by: `TestImageBytesAreNotTheLongestText`, `TestMountNodeKindsAreTheViewVocabulary`, `TestMountImageIsPreparedOffTheLoopAndTransmittedOnce`, `TestMountImageUpdateSecondMountAndEnd`, `TestMountImageIsNoTallerThanItsSlot`, `TestMountImageRowsPerTerminal`, `TestViewClientDeclaresTheImageNodeAndItsFormats`, `TestViewClientDrawsAPhotographInAPanel`, `TestViewKittyPlaceholdersByEnvironment`.
- Grid node, added 2026-11-12 (the owner ordered calendar-grade layout into 0.8.0; the owner approved the node kind on 2026-11-12T23:22:43Z in the words "proceed with recommendations", which the decision log records as "New view contract members APPROVED: grid node, mount title, whenHidden intent, focus request: update + 'unfocused' notice, two live terminal panels as tabs, request: mount does not take focus from an editor holding text, no declared node kinds = none drawn", relayed through Main). The view vocabulary gains `grid`: `{kind: "grid", id, columns (1..16, equal width), head? (as many labels as columns), chipRows? (1..8, default 3), selected?, cells [{value?, label?, mark? ("current"), dim?, color?, chips [{text, color?}] (at most 8), more? (0..9999)}]}`, at most 128 cells and 32 rows, the last row may be short, cell values unique and `selected` one of them. Cells are data, not nodes: a month with three chips a day is three nodes (the view, a title and the grid), not the 281 to 407 of nested stacks. The sender lists the events it shows as `chips` and counts the ones it left out in `more`; the client draws at most `chipRows` chip lines in a cell (fewer when its slot is short) and, when events are left over, a line "+K more" where K is the chips not drawn plus `more`. Choosing a cell (keyboard, click or tap) raises the intent whose `nodeKey` is the grid's `id` with the cell's `value`, as a select-list does for an item. A cell colour and a chip colour follow the colour rule of a drawing (`#rrggbb`, `#rrggbbaa` or a theme token), because calendar colours are the user's data and not the theme's; the `bg` and `color` members of other nodes take theme tokens only and are unchanged. The record is closed: a member the grid, a cell or a chip does not have, or one past any bound, rejects the call with `invalid`. The terminal draws a grid in equal columns of `(width - separators) / columns` cells cut by terminal cells, marks today by bold inverse video, puts a chip on its colour with the black or white foreground of the higher WCAG contrast and a leading glyph, and takes the arrow keys, Home, End, Enter and Space while the grid holds the focus (the panel the focus record names); every other key, and a move past an edge, is left to the client. The later span form (a week or a day as timelines) extends the same kind with `spans`, `rowLabels` and `marker` and never with a member named `items`. Every SDK builds the node and none checks, defaults or drops a member, so the host's `checkGrid` stays the one validator: Go `kit.Grid`, `kit.GridCell` and `kit.GridChip`, Rust `kit::Grid`, `kit::GridCell` and `kit::GridChip`, Python `kit.Grid`, `kit.GridCell` and `kit.GridChip`, Lisp `kit-grid`, `kit-grid-cell` and `kit-grid-chip`, and in Node the plain object, typed by `PiGViewGrid`, `PiGViewGridCell` and `PiGViewGridChip`; a member the author does not set is absent on the wire.
- Interactive endpoint, added 2026-11-13 (owner direction of 2026-11-13T00:54:22Z: the hero demo, one extension in the TUI and the web client at once, is a release gate; the owner's D1 Option A of 2026-11-12 names its parts: the TUI view client, a carrier socket on an interactive session (owner, 2026-11-12T19:40Z: "a flag-gated interactive carrier socket"; in the question put to him: "N2 'pig --ui-view-listen <socket>' (explicit flag)"), a bridge that connects; Main's ruling of 2026-11-13T01:12Z: build the endpoint under this amendment, inert in Stock PiG, selected by a Piglet, with no new message, carrier member or SDK member). The Piglet key `viewEndpoint` takes `none` (the default, Stock PiG) or `socket`; a child Piglet inherits it and replaces it by naming it. The endpoint is turned on by that Piglet key and by no command-line flag: the owner's words name a flag, `pig --ui-view-listen`, and PiG has none, because a Stock capability is selected by a Piglet (AGENTS.md, 'Product-extension boundary'); `Main` in `coding/cli/main.go` takes the key from the active Piglet. With `socket`, an interactive session serves the wire carrier above on a Unix socket, `<agent directory>/view-endpoint/<pid>.sock`, for as long as an extension host is bound to it. Every connection is one carrier onto the registry of that session, so the clients of the socket and the terminal client hold one registry: each is sent every mount resolved for its own abilities, and an intent any of them raises is answered once and its redraw delivered to all. Print, JSON and RPC mode ignore the key. Who may connect: this user only. The directory is created with mode 0700 and the socket set to 0600; the directory is used only when it is a directory (not a link) of mode 0700 that this user owns, and a path already at the socket's name is removed only when it is this user's own socket of mode 0600, otherwise no socket is served and one warning says why; the sockets of processes that no longer exist are removed by the same rule. The kernel's record of each peer's user (`SO_PEERCRED` on Linux, `LOCAL_PEERCRED` on macOS) is compared with this process's user before a byte is read, and a peer of another user is closed, because a file system may ignore modes. On Windows and every other system the session starts, no socket is served and one warning line says so. What a client can do: the seven carrier commands and nothing else. Any other line, a command of RPC mode included, gets `{"type":"response","command":<type>,"success":false,"error":"Unknown command: <type>"}` and changes nothing, so a client of the socket can draw mounts and raise the intents they declare, and cannot prompt, read the session or change a setting. Lifetime: when the extension host is unbound (a session replacement by new, resume, fork or switch, the exit, a signal that ends pig, the loss of the terminal) every connection is closed and the socket is removed; the next host is served under the same path, with a registry of its own. A reload keeps the host and its registry, so the connections and their attachments stay: a client is sent what the registry delivers, as the terminal is, which at a reload is the end of the reloaded extensions' mounts (the reload rule below). A client is told by the end of its connection, connects again and attaches; no client is left attached to a host that is no longer served, and an intent in flight has no reply. A process that is killed leaves its socket, which the next start in that agent directory removes. Bounds: the registry's 8 attachments count the terminal and the socket's clients together, so the ninth attach fails `limit`; 16 connections at once (every attachment and as many more that connect to be told `limit`), one more is closed as it is accepted; a line longer than the carrier's 4194304 bytes closes the connection; a connection that is owed 512 lines or 8388608 bytes it has not been written (twice the carrier's queue of 256 events and 4194304 bytes: a full queue of events and as many replies) is closed. Events never reach that bound, because the carrier hands over one queue and then waits, so a client that stops reading is owed the carrier's queue, then one `ui_view_gap`; only replies to commands a client keeps sending unread can. The next command of a connection is read once the reply to the last was written. Cost: the interactive loop only queues a bind or an unbind; one worker goroutine creates and removes the socket and ends when it has nothing to do, and each connection has one reader and one writer, which alone waits on the client. Without the key no directory is made, nothing listens and no goroutine runs. Locked by: `TestClientsOfTheSocketShareTheRegistryOfTheSession`, `TestOnlyCarrierCommandsAreServed`, `TestAPeerOfAnotherUserIsClosedBeforeALineIsRead`, `TestTheDirectoryAndTheSocketMustBeThisUsersOwn`, `TestUnbindClosesEveryConnectionAndCloseLeavesNothing`, `TestAnUnboundEndpointTouchesNothing`, `TestASocketPathLongerThanAnAddressHoldsIsRefused`, `TestALineLongerThanTheCarriersBoundClosesTheConnection`, `TestTheNinthAttachmentIsRefusedAndTheSeventeenthConnectionClosed`, `TestAClientThatDoesNotReadHoldsNoOtherClient`, `TestRepliesNobodyReadsCutTheConnection`, `TestRepliesNobodyReadsCutTheConnectionWhileTheWriterIsHeld`, `TestAClientThatConnectsWhileTheHostIsUnboundIsNeverLeftAttached`, `TestCloseDoesNotWaitForAClientThatDoesNotRead` and `TestNoSocketIsServedOnThisSystem` (`internal/viewendpoint`), `TestParse_ViewEndpoint`, `TestViewEndpointInheritsAndIsReplaced`, `TestStandardServesNoViewEndpoint`, and in a real session under tmux `TestUIViewEndpointAndTerminalShareOneSession`, `TestUIViewEndpointClientThatDoesNotReadDoesNotHoldTheTerminal`, `TestUIViewEndpointReloadKeepsAndNewSessionClosesTheConnection`, `TestUIViewEndpointSocketIsRemovedAtExit` and `TestUIViewEndpointIsSelectedByThePigletAndInteractiveOnly`.
- Reload rule, added 2026-11-13 (Main's ruling of 2026-11-13 on the approved text, with two rules it rests on: this repository's extension contract, "Reload and shutdown remove owned handlers and work", and Pi's reload, which removes everything an extension drew before the extensions load again: `interactive-mode.ts` `handleReloadCommand` calls `resetExtensionUI`). A mount belongs to the extension instance that made it. A reload replaces the instance of every extension it loads again, in every realization and whether or not the extension's process is kept, so `Host.Reload`, once its configuration has loaded and before it starts a replacement, ends every mount of the loaded extensions as `broken` with reason `owner_gone`: every attached client is delivered each such mount once more, in name order of the extensions. The instance the reload loads mounts its keys again under a new epoch; before this rule its mount failed with `invalid` ("already mounted") while the registry still held the mount of the instance before. An intent raised on the ended mount is refused (`mount is broken`) and reaches no handler. The connection of an instance that is being replaced mounts nothing more (`terminal`), so no mount outlives its instance. The rule is the host's, so it holds wherever a client can attach (interactive and RPC mode); in print and JSON mode `mountView` is refused with `unsupported`, so no mount exists there; it adds no record, reason or message. Locked by: `TestConformance_UIViewMountsEndAtAReload` (Node, Go, Python and Rust, isolated and packed, and Lisp packed), `TestRPCUIViewReloadBreaksTheMountsOfTheReloadedExtension` (a carrier client over RPC, `ctx.reload()`), and in a real session under tmux, the terminal and a client of the socket both, `TestUIViewEndpointReloadEndsTheMountsOfTheReloadedExtensions` (a Node extension with its source unchanged and changed, the Go fixture in a process, and the fixture's factory form fused into a Piglet Binary) and `TestUIViewEndpointExtensionProcessEndBreaksItsMounts`.
- A client's notice when a mount ends, added 2026-11-13. A client says nothing when a plugin ends its own mount; for a broken mount it may say so once, and withdraws that when the key is mounted again. Owner decision entry of 2026-11-13T18:42:07Z (owner decisions of 0.8.0) records this wording among the recommendations he accepted. It rests on the reload rule above: at a reload every mount of a reloaded extension reaches a client once more as `broken` with reason `owner_gone`, and the new instance's mount of the same key follows under a higher epoch, so a notice that stayed would stand under a view that is live again. The reference web client (`examples/ui-view-web`) does this: no line for a `finished` or `removed` mount, and one line by the mount's title for a broken mount it had drawn, which it removes when the key is mounted again (`TestPageSaysNothingOfAMountItsExtensionEndedOrMountedAgain`).
- Not taken: a version or negotiation field anywhere, a fixed claim registry, palettes and opaque drawing payloads, operation-envelope records, assets by reference (they need a host asset store) and a carrier inside pig for any protocol other than RPC mode's lines: a program that speaks another protocol relays these lines (`docs/ui-view-carrier.md`, "Relaying the carrier over another protocol"). A Go embedder attaches through `uiview.Registry.Attach` on `UIBridge.Views()` of `coding/extension/host/subprocess`; `TestConformance_UIViewMounts` drives that path end to end. The `button` node of the D614 catalog is not built yet; it is the node A2UI's Button maps to.

Pi source: none (Pi's extension UI is in-process `Component` objects). Cancellation follows `AbortSignal` on a handler's `ctx.signal`.

Why: the owner approved one protocol for terminal, web, desktop and ACP clients on 2026-11-09 (multilang-plugins D614). The records are PiG's own and unversioned; no code, schema text or identifier is copied from another project (the provenance guard is `automation/ci/check-public-hygiene.py`).

Stock behavior: no client attached, so a mount fails with `unsupported` and nothing is drawn. Existing extensions, Pi's wire and the parity scenarios are unchanged: `ui.view.*` calls and `uiAbilities` exist only for an extension that uses them, and the dependency is one-way (`make gui-import-guard`: chord never imports `coding/uiview`). A client that sends none of the carrier's seven commands is written Pi's bytes: the rpc parity family is unchanged, and `TestRPCUIViewNeverAttachedClientSeesPiBytes` compares a session whose extension mounts a view with the same session without the mount. An interactive session with no Piglet, or with a Piglet that does not set `viewClient`, attaches no client. With `viewClient: terminal` and no mount, it writes the terminal the bytes it writes without the key (`TestTerminalViewClientNeverMountedCostsNothing`), and once a mount ended it draws what a session that never mounted draws (`TestTerminalViewClientMountThenUnmountLeavesNoTrace`, `TestUIViewMountThenUnmountIsTheSessionThatNeverMounted`).

Call-site markers: `coding/uiview/uiview.go` (the registry and its limits), `coding/uiview/claims.go`, `coding/uiview/drawing.go`, `coding/uiview/notice.go`, `coding/uiview/attach.go`, `coding/uiview/canonical.go`, `coding/uiview/bindings.go` (`ReservedKeys`, `ParseBindingKey`), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/protocol.go` (`CallUIViewMount` and the other `ui.view.*` constants, `CallUIViewDiagnostics`, `RequestUIViewIntent`, `StatePayload.UIAbilities`), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/ui_view_mount.go`, `extensions/sdk/ui_view.go`, `extensions/sdk-py/pig_sdk/ui_view.py`, `extensions/sdk-rs/src/ui_view.rs`, `extensions/sdk-lisp/src/ui-view.lisp`, `coding/extension/host/subprocess/runtime-node/runtime.mjs` (`viewAbilities`, `mountView`, `finishView`, `viewDiagnostics`, `onViewIntent`), `extensions/sdk-ts/index.d.ts`. ## D621 Interactive runs keep a heap goal of at least 16 MiB, at GOGC 150 until the live heap passes 8 MiB

Summary: An interactive run keeps a heap goal of at least 16 MiB, at a garbage-collection percent of 150 until 8 MiB is live and the default 100 after, unless you set GOGC or GOMEMLIMIT.

Audience: runtime

Stock disposition: inert capability. It selects no product behavior, and a user's `GOGC` or `GOMEMLIMIT` turns it off: the terminal output, session files and model requests are the same; only when the Go collector runs changes.

What: when the environment sets neither `GOGC` nor `GOMEMLIMIT`, `cli.Main` applies `gcpolicy.Interactive` to an interactive run (not `--listen`, D232) before the session loads, through D175's live-heap watcher. The rule: (1) after each collection the next heap goal is the larger of 16 MiB and the goal of the run's percent; (2) the run's percent is 150 until a collection finds more than 8 MiB live, and 100 from then on for the rest of the process; (3) so the floor decides while less than about 6 MiB is live, or 7.5 MiB once the percent is 100, which a heap is again after it has shrunk, and above that the goal is the percent's alone, as before the floor; (4) a watcher that goes 2 s without a collection sets the percent to 100 and ends, so a session left at its prompt fires no timer, and a new watcher starts at the next collection; (5) a `GOGC` or `GOMEMLIMIT` in the environment, or a `--listen` server, keeps the runtime's own settings. The watcher sets the floor through the percent: after a collection that left `live` bytes of heap and `scan` bytes of stacks and globals it sets the percent `(16 MiB - live) / (live + scan)`, never under the run's percent and never over 400, the percent whose minimum heap is 16 MiB; the process starts at 400. It reads `/gc/heap/live:bytes` every 5 ms, backing off while no collection finishes. D175's print, json and rpc policy is unchanged (400 until 64 MiB, GOGC only, no idle stop).

Idle: a watcher that goes 2 s without a collection sets the percent to 100 and ends. A cleanup attached to a sentinel object starts a new watcher after the next collection, and a stop of the policy ends that restart. A prompt that follows a pause of 2 s or more therefore begins at the default goal, floor or not, and the collection that follows restarts the policy; the floor's gain is for prompts that come within 2 s of a collection. A change that leaves the percent at the idle stop and stops reading after 0.3 s was measured with the floor (below) and is not part of this record.

Pi source: none. Node's collector has no such setting; the GC target is a Go runtime choice.

Why: an interactive run's cost is dominated by short bursts of allocation (a turn streams, a card renders) over a heap of a few megabytes, and the default target spends collections on it: 21 cycles in 20 prompts of a long session, about 3.7 ms each. The percent of 150, measured in one harness invocation, paired by round, on pinned xd670 cores (base 7750686238dd vs GOGC 150 until 8 MiB, n = 8): the interactive scenario's CPU 205.7 -> 191.5 ms (-6.6 %, 8 of 8 pairs), the default-theme scenario's CPU 89.9 -> 83.9 ms (-8.0 %, 8 of 8), time to interactive -13 %; in a 20-prompt session CPU per prompt -3.4 % and user instructions -4.2 % (8 of 8), at 40 prompts CPU -3.3 % and instructions -2.2 % (8 of 8), for 1.6 MB more peak own memory in the interactive scenario. A percent sets a goal in proportion to the live heap, so with 3 to 5 MiB live it still collects every 5 to 8 MiB allocated. The floor, measured the same way against the release head 31bd78d54f (one invocation per scenario, 13 to 16 rounds, 9 to 15 pairs after the runs with foreign load on the pinned cores are dropped): the interactive scenario runs 15.3 % fewer user instructions (12 of 12 pairs) and its CPU falls 176.8 -> 164.8 ms (-6.5 %, 14 of 14), time to interactive -12.3 %; the default-theme scenario's CPU 76.1 -> 69.5 ms (-8.3 %, 15 of 15) and a one-prompt session 18.4 % fewer user instructions; a 20-prompt session 2.9 % fewer user instructions per prompt and a 40-prompt session 1.7 % fewer (13 of 13 each), with no change in CPU per prompt beyond the difference between two runs of one binary. The 2 s idle rule is unchanged and was measured with the floor (n = 5 to 13 pairs): in a session of five prompts with 3 s between them the floor alone runs 8.5 % fewer user instructions; in the 20 s after an answer it reads 0.0088 ms/s as the base does (no doubling, n = 10 to 11); over two minutes it reads 0.006 ms/s against 0.004 (higher in 5 of 5 pairs, +37 %, about 0.19 ms of CPU per two minutes; the cause was not found). A change that keeps the percent at the idle stop and stops reading after 0.3 s instead was measured with the floor: five prompts with 3 s pauses 18.2 % fewer user instructions (the idle change alone 9.3 %), a session at its prompt not scheduled once in 20 s, 0.9 MB more peak own memory with pauses; it awaits the owner's decision and is not part of this record. The rows of the floor above were measured on a build that had that idle change; the floor alone (this record's commit) was measured in two other invocations against the same head: the interactive scenario's CPU 174 -> 161 ms (11 of 14 pairs below, inside the spread of two runs of one binary), time to interactive -12.3 %, peak own memory 16.8 -> 20.2 MB (highest run 20.5); a 20-prompt and a 40-prompt session 3.2 % and 1.6 % fewer user instructions per prompt; five prompts without pauses 14.5 % fewer user instructions and 7.2 % less CPU. The cost is memory: the five-prompt scenario's peak own memory rises from 16.8 to 20.3 MB (highest run 20.5; Pi-Bolt 26.6), and with 3 s between prompts from 12.4 to 12.6 MB. A session at its prompt holds 0.1 MB more own memory, and the Go runtime 1.1 to 1.2 MB more that it has not returned to the operating system (17.05 to 17.09 MB against 15.92 MB in the tmux idle gate, whose ceiling is 17.85 MB). A session's own memory after 20 and 40 prompts is unchanged (25.1 -> 24.9 MB and 35.7 -> 35.5 MB, inside the noise): its heap is past the floor by then. GOGC 200 would raise the interactive peak to 20.6 MB for 193.6 ms, and a GC-off policy with a runtime memory limit (16, 20 and 24 MiB, switching at half the limit) was measured and dropped: 16 and 20 MiB collected so often that a long session's CPU per prompt rose 50-80 %, and 24 MiB raised peak memory more (+7 MB) than this policy. The floor is not that policy: the collector stays on its percent pacing and only its goal has a lower bound. A floor of 12 MiB gives the same in a one-prompt session (17.8 % fewer user instructions), 11.2 % against 15.3 % in the interactive scenario without raising its peak own memory (16.6 MB), and nothing measurable in a long session. A floor of 24 MiB was measured too: it saves more in long sessions (8.2 % and 4.4 % fewer user instructions per prompt at 20 and 40 prompts) and raises the interactive scenario's peak own memory to 27.6 MB, above Pi-Bolt's 26.6.

Call-site markers: `internal/gcpolicy/gcpolicy.go` (`Interactive`, `Policy.Apply`, `Policy.percentFor`, `Policy.watchLiveHeap`, `watcher.resumeAtNextCollection`), `coding/cli/gc_policy.go` (`gcPolicyFor`), `coding/cli/main.go` (`Main`).

Locked by: `TestInteractiveHeapGoalByLiveHeap` (the rule: live heaps of 0 to 20 MiB against the runtime's heap goal, before and after the 8 MiB read and after the heap has shrunk), `TestGoalFloorPercent`, `TestInteractiveKeepsItsGoalFloorWhileTheHeapIsSmall`, `TestInteractiveGoalFloorHoldsAgainAfterTheHeapShrinks`, `TestInteractiveRevertsAtItsLiveHeapThreshold`, `TestBatchKeepsItsTargetBetweenTheInteractiveAndBatchThresholds`, `TestInteractiveStopsWhenIdle` (idle stop, restart at the next collection, stop ends the restart), `TestInteractiveLeavesAUserGOGCOrMemoryLimit`, `TestBatchIgnoresAMemoryLimit` (`internal/gcpolicy`), `TestGCPolicyFollowsTheRunMode` (`coding/cli`: interactive gets the interactive policy, print, json and rpc D175's, `--listen` none).

Remove when: the Go runtime's default pacing stops collecting small interactive heaps, or a measurement shows GOGC 100 costs no CPU on the interactive and default-theme scenarios.

Approval: owner, 2026-11-12T14:42Z (`handoffs/0.8.0/OWNER-DECISIONS.md`): "D621 16 MiB floor approved if the complete numbers hold"; the Revision below says how that condition was met.
By owner principle (decision 29, 2026-11-17), checked by Claude and Tern
---

Revision (minimum heap goal 16 MiB): approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-11-12T14:42Z, through the owner's side: "D621 16 MiB floor approved if the complete numbers hold". The condition held: interactive CPU 18.6 % below Pi-Bolt (from 12.9 %), default theme 15.5 % below (from 7.9 %), interactive peak own memory 16.1 -> 20.3 MB against the bar of 22.44 MB; measured on shared cores, to be repeated on the clean host for the scoreboard. Those numbers were measured at 5c8175fb9095, whose policy has the 0.3 s idle form (`InteractiveIdle = 300 * time.Millisecond`), not the 2 s form this record describes. On the release line the 2 s form as landed (arm `base`, line tip f14a46e87fb7) was compared with the 0.3 s form picked onto it (arm `idle`) on 2026-11-13T15:43Z (16 rounds, interactive scenario; xd6702 `~/lanes/GcIdleOnLine.out/m2-int.agg.txt`, arms named in `m2.sh`): CPU 79.75 against 79.87 ms (0.1 %, inside the spread), peak own memory 19.7 against 19.6 MB (bar 22.44 MB), time to interactive 15.22 against 15.30 ms: the condition holds for the landed form. The idle change measured beside the floor is not part of this approval.
SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:b5aea8733275674f

## D625 terminal.framePacing "adaptive" begins a streaming reply's next frame 10 to 16 ms after the last, by what that frame cost

Summary: With `terminal.framePacing: "adaptive"` and the opt-in screen writer (D624) on, a streaming reply's frames begin 10 to 16 ms apart in fullscreen mode on listed terminals; otherwise 16 ms, as Pi's.

Audience: runtime

Stock disposition: inert capability. It selects no product behavior: with the setting unset or `pi` and the variable naming no policy, the renderer is given no policy (`installFramePacing`) and every frame interval is Pi's constant; with it on, what a frame shows is the same and only when frames begin differs.

What: Pi draws a requested frame at once when 16 ms have passed since the last frame began, and otherwise when they have (`packages/tui/src/tui.ts` `requestRender`, `scheduleRender`, `MIN_RENDER_INTERVAL_MS = 16`); a forced request and a key press draw at once. PiG keeps that rule and, under the adaptive frame pacing policy, changes only the interval: `min(16 ms, max(10 ms, 4 x c))`, where c is what the last frame took from its start to the return of its write. A frame may therefore begin up to 6 ms sooner than Pi's; what a frame shows is what Pi's frame would show at that moment. A frame that cost 4 ms or more, which includes one whose write the terminal blocked, is followed by Pi's interval.

Every frame is measured, a forced one and a key press too: they are never delayed, and their cost is the c of the interval after them. The interval never exceeds Pi's 16 ms, so the frame after a slow frame begins no later than 16 ms after the slow one began (or when it ended, if it took longer), as under Pi's rule, and the slow frame's cost is replaced by the next frame's.

The policy answers only when all of these hold; otherwise the interval is Pi's constant: the run selected it (`terminal.framePacing: "adaptive"` in the global settings file; the key unset, `pi` and any other value are Pi's interval, which is the default, decided in the one constant `terminalFramePacingDefault`; `PIG_FRAME_PACING=pi|0|adaptive` wins over the setting, and any other value of it counts as unset; both are read once, at start); the screen writer (D624) is active, which it is only when the run selected it (the writer is opt-in in 0.8.0: `terminal.screenWriter: "writer"` or `PIG_TUI_SCREEN_WRITER=writer`; a run that selects the policy without the writer has Pi's interval, and interactive mode says so once at each start, as a warning in the transcript after the startup diagnostics (`showFramePacingNotice`): `Warning: Adaptive frame pacing (terminal.framePacing, PIG_FRAME_PACING) has no effect without the screen writer (terminal.screenWriter "writer"): frames keep Pi's 16 ms interval.` Print, JSON and RPC mode read neither setting and write nothing of it, on stdout or stderr) and only on the alternate screen, so only in fullscreen mode; the terminal is trusted with synchronized updates (below); an agent run is active and no compaction is running; no extension dialog, overlay, header, footer, editor or widget is mounted, no overlay is open, and the transcript holds no tool card, custom message or custom entry that an extension's renderer draws (from the frame after such a component is added until the transcript is next emptied; a tool renderer resolver counts when it answers for the tool, which the built-in MCP cards' resolver does).

Trusted terminals: a terminal is trusted when D205's list trusts it with scroll regions, the environment shows no tmux, GNU screen, Zellij or Herdr in front of it, and the environment names one of the terminals below by a name Pi's capability detection reads (`tui/terminal_image.go`). A terminal that names itself in `TERM_PROGRAM` is judged by that name alone (a variable inherited from another terminal decides nothing): an unlisted name is not trusted, and a listed one is not trusted when `TERM_PROGRAM_VERSION` states a release older than the first with mode 2026. A version that is missing or does not begin with a number says nothing, except for the VS Code terminal, which must state 1.108 or later. tmux 3.4 and 3.6 answer a mode 2026 request (`CSI ? 2026 $ p`) with nothing (probed on the lane host and in the CI image, 2026-11-12).

| terminal | named by | first release with mode 2026 | source of the release | states its version |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| kitty | `KITTY_WINDOW_ID`, `TERM_PROGRAM=kitty` | 0.22.0 | repository tags: the implementing commit 5768c54c ("Add support for pending mode via SM/RM 2026") is in `v0.22.0` and not in `v0.21.2`; no changelog line | no (`kitty/child.py` sets no `TERM_PROGRAM`): by name |
| Ghostty | `TERM` containing `ghostty`, `GHOSTTY_RESOURCES_DIR`, `TERM_PROGRAM=ghostty` | 1.0.0, its first public release | repository tags: the implementing commit 2cc1e437 ("termio: handle all the synchronized output setting, timer") is in `v1.0.0`; no release-note line | yes (`src/termio/Exec.zig`): below 1.0.0 is not trusted |
| WezTerm | `WEZTERM_PANE`, `TERM_PROGRAM=WezTerm` | 20210814-124438-54e29167 | its changelog: "Added support for Synchronized Rendering" (#882) | yes (`config/src/config.rs`): below 20210814 is not trusted |
| Warp | `WARP_SESSION_ID`, `WARP_TERMINAL_SESSION_UUID`, `TERM_PROGRAM=WarpTerminal` | v0.2025.01.15.08.02.stable_02 | a maintainer's statement on warpdotdev/Warp#2185 (2025-01-16), not a changelog line | not confirmed from a source; when it does, below v0.2025.01.15.08.02 is not trusted |
| iTerm2 | `ITERM_SESSION_ID`, `TERM_PROGRAM=iTerm.app` | 3.5.0 (first in 3.5.0beta1) | its release notes: "Add DECSET 2026 for synchronized updates"; the commit f7efeb4bbaa5 is in neither `v3.4.9` nor `v3.4.10` | yes (since 2016): below 3.5.0 is not trusted |
| Alacritty | `TERM` beginning with `alacritty`, `ALACRITTY_WINDOW_ID`, `TERM_PROGRAM=alacritty` | 0.13.0 | its changelog: "Synchronized updates now use `CSI 2026` instead of legacy `DCS` variant" | no (`alacritty_terminal/src/tty/mod.rs` sets `TERM` and `COLORTERM` only): by name |
| VS Code terminal | `TERM_PROGRAM=vscode` with `TERM_PROGRAM_VERSION` 1.108 or later | 1.108 (December 2025) | its release notes: "Synchronized output" (microsoft/vscode#284592) | yes: required, because releases without the mode are common |

Not listed though Pi's detection names it: Windows Terminal (`WT_SESSION`). Mode 2026 is in Preview 1.24.2372.0 (2025-08-26) and in stable 1.23.20211.0 (2026-01-24, a backport), by its release notes (microsoft/terminal#18826); `WT_SESSION` carries no version and the stable release is recent, so nothing tells an installation with the mode from one without.

Known residual, terminals named without a version: a kitty or Alacritty older than the release above, a terminal reached only by `KITTY_WINDOW_ID`, `GHOSTTY_RESOURCES_DIR`, `WEZTERM_PANE`, the Warp or iTerm2 session variables or `TERM`, and a Warp that states no version are trusted by name. On a release without mode 2026 they get the frames PiG writes anyway, each inside the mode 2026 brackets that Pi also writes around every frame and that such a terminal ignores, up to 100 a second where Pi's interval gives 62; the terminal shows a frame's cells as they arrive, as it does with Pi's frames. A run that does not select the policy has Pi's interval.

Frames that send nothing: a frame the screen writer sends nothing of (it changed no cell, cursor position or cursor visibility) does not restart the interval. `ProcessTerminal` counts the writes it sends on, and the scheduler reads the count before and after a frame (`renderAndReschedule`, through the unexported `screenWriterTerminal` interface; two atomic loads, no lock). While the last frame that showed something is younger than Pi's 16 ms, the next frame is due four times the empty frame's cost after it began, at least 1 ms (`emptyFrameWait`), twice that after the second empty frame in a row and four times after the third, never sooner than the adaptive interval after the frame that showed something and never later than 16 ms after it. From then on the interval is Pi's 16 ms after the last frame, until a frame shows something. So a change is shown no later than Pi's interval would show it after the same frame, and a stream that changes nothing on the screen (a reply streaming below the viewport of a transcript scrolled up) is drawn as often as Pi draws it after at most three frames more: in one second of requests 1 ms apart 65 frames where Pi's interval draws 62, the three once per stretch, not per interval. Without this rule the change that follows an empty frame waited a whole 10 ms more: in one logged run of a 50 KB file write on the benchmark host, 219 of the 769 frames the renderer drew sent nothing to the terminal and 204 of the 543 gaps between visible changes were 18 to 22 ms, where Pi's interval had none. A first form of the rule (one free frame, then Pi's interval) passed its clock tests and failed on a stream that asks for a frame every 2 ms and changes the screen every 12 ms (38 of 63 visible gaps of 18 ms or more), because the request after an empty frame usually changes nothing either; that stream is `TestAdaptivePacingDoesNotWaitOutAFrameThatSentNothing`.

Updates that change nothing: Pi's `message_update` handler asks for a frame on every update (`packages/coding-agent/src/modes/interactive/interactive-mode.ts`, `case "message_update"` at line 3649, `this.ui.requestRender()` at line 3681), and PiG's does the same in two calls (`internal/codingagent/interactive_events.go`, `agent.MessageUpdateEvent`). While the adaptive policy paces the frames (`framePacingInForce`: installed, gate open, the renderer's `FramePacingActive`), a tool call delta whose card says that its lines are those its last frame drew asks for none (`ToolExecutionComponent.CallShowsNoChange`, `ToolDefinitionRenderers.CallUnchanged`). Only the built-in write card says so (`writeCallShowsNoChange`), from the state its last draw left: the card is collapsed, the arguments are incomplete, the path is the same, the content extends a line the last draw already counted, without a new line, the content is past the 50 lines of the leading block that is highlighted again on every update, and that block is highlighted for the current epoch. Every other card, an extension's renderer and any doubt are a change. A write call streams a few characters per update and a collapsed write card past its first ten lines changes only when a line begins, so without this most frames the policy drew during a file write found nothing to send. Under Pi's interval both calls run on every update exactly as before (`TestToolCallDeltaAsksForNoFrameWhenTheCardShowsNoChange`: two requests per delta with no policy, with the gate closed and with the terminal's conditions unmet). The start after 50 lines is conservative, not shown necessary: a mutation that starts after 11 lines survives the property test, whose streams hold comments and template strings closed many lines later or never; it is not widened.

Pi source: `packages/tui/src/tui.ts` (`requestRender`, `scheduleRender`, `MIN_RENDER_INTERVAL_MS = 16`). Pi has one interval and no setting for it.

Why: on a terminal that shows a frame at once a fast stream can be drawn sooner than every 16 ms. Measured on the benchmark host (xd6702, NUMA node 1, quad Q6: client, model, harness and tmux on cores 161, 163, 165 and 167; the kit's `abx_run2.py` called directly with `--perf off`, without sudo, so no instruction counts and no scheduler trace; terminal declared as Alacritty; the screen writer on in every arm, which was the default in these binaries and is opt-in since; 10 measured rounds and 1 warm-up per arm, 44 runs, none dropped; binaries of commit c638eefe4; medians of 10, the policy against Pi's interval run twice, `pi` and `pi2`, whose difference is the floor). Streamed answer (`md:20000`): 194 frames against 121 and 120; time between visible changes p50 9.67 against 15.58 and 15.59 ms, p95 10.40 against 16.40 and 16.47, p99 11.94 against 17.11 and 17.27; longest pause 13.6 against 19.1 and 18.7 ms; CPU time 0.263 against 0.252 and 0.246 s. File write of 50 KB (`write:50`): 538 frames against 467 and 467; p50 13.41 against 15.56 and 15.55 ms, p95 15.74 against 15.99 and 15.99, p99 18.88 against 16.54 and 17.87; longest pause 55.2 against 53.0 and 58.9 ms; CPU time 0.897 against 0.857 and 0.853 s. File write of 200 KB (`write:200`): 2072 frames against 1844 and 1846; p50 13.51 against 15.55 and 15.56 ms, p95 15.80 against 15.97 and 15.96, p99 16.81 against 16.27 and 16.23; longest pause 81.7 against 90.1 and 81.8 ms; CPU time 3.437 against 3.278 and 3.303 s. The bar for the default was: p95 and p99 on the two write cells no worse than Pi's beyond the floor, the longest pause no longer, CPU time within the floor. Met: p95 in both write cells (0.25 and 0.17 ms under), the longest pause in all three cells, p99 on `write:50` (1.01 ms over with a floor of 1.33). Missed: p99 on `write:200` (0.54 ms over with a floor of 0.04) and CPU time (4.7 to 5.2 % over on `write:50`, 4.1 to 4.9 % on `write:200`, 4.1 to 6.7 % on `md:20000`, with floors of 0.5, 0.8 and 2.4 %), most of it the 15 % more frames the policy draws. So the policy is opt-in and Pi's interval is the default. How it got there, same host and method: before frames that send nothing were handled, `write:50` had p95 19.84 and p99 20.14 ms with 36 % of the gaps at 18 to 22 ms; with that rule alone p95 15.68 but p99 34.51 ms and 18 % more CPU time, because more than half of the frames drawn sent nothing (746 of 1347 in one logged run); with updates that change nothing asking for no frame, the numbers above (in one logged run 638 frames drawn, 96 of which sent nothing; Pi's interval 483 and 9). With instruction counts, in a later run of the streamed answer cell (xd6702, 2026-11-13, quad Q6, the kit's `abx2.sh` with `perf stat`, terminal declared as Alacritty, the screen writer on in both arms as that binary's default, 20 measured rounds per arm, binary of the release line at 926e3dc30 merged with c638eefe4; medians): the policy drew 188 visible frames where Pi's interval drew 114, with 9.29 against 15.46 ms at p50 and 9.98 against 15.99 ms at p99 between visible changes, for 4.70e8 user-mode instructions against 4.07e8, 15 % more: 2.50 million a frame against 3.57 million, so the policy's instruction cost is the price of the frames it adds, not a dearer frame. Not covered: a stream whose updates change nothing on the screen for another reason, such as a reply streaming below the viewport of a transcript scrolled up, is drawn as the rule for frames that send nothing says, 65 frames against 62 in a second; the kit has no cell for it. Not measured: the kit's interactive and long cells, a real terminal other than the declared one, macOS.

Scope: `tui` (`FramePacing`, `frameIntervalLocked`, `synchronizedUpdatesTrustedIn`, `synchronizedUpdatesRelease`; `TuiAltScreen` on a `ProcessTerminal` with the screen writer takes part, every other renderer keeps Pi's constant) and `internal/codingagent` (`installFramePacing`, `allowFramePacing`, `replyStreamsUndisturbed`, `extensionDrawsOnScreen`, `Settings.TerminalFramePacing`, `terminalFramePacingDefault`; the startup warning: `showFramePacingNotice`, `adaptiveFramePacingLacksScreenWriter`, which asks `tui.ScreenWriterSelected` for the screen writer's own selection rule). Print, JSON and RPC mode have no renderer with the policy and show no warning.

Call-site markers: `tui/frame_pacing.go` (`FramePacing`, `frameIntervalFloor`, `emptyFrameWait`, `frameCostFactor`, `frameIntervalLocked`), `tui/tui_alt_screen_scroll.go` (`synchronizedUpdatesTrusted`, `synchronizedUpdatesRelease`), `internal/codingagent/frame_pacing.go` (`installFramePacing`, `showFramePacingNotice`, `replyStreamsUndisturbed`, `extensionRenderersMounted`), `internal/codingagent/tool_definition_renderers.go` (`resolveToolRenderers`), `internal/codingagent/interactive_events.go` (the `agent.MessageUpdateEvent` case), `internal/codingagent/tool_builtin_renderers.go` (`writeCallShowsNoChange`), `tui/tool_execution_definition.go` (`CallShowsNoChange`), `internal/codingagent/settings.go` (`TerminalFramePacing`, `terminalFramePacingDefault`, `GetTerminalFramePacing`).

Locked by: `tui/frame_pacing_test.go` on a manual clock: a frame that sent nothing does not restart the interval and the wait after it is four times its cost, 1 ms at least (`TestFrameThatSentNothingDoesNotRestartTheInterval`), doubles with each further one and ends with Pi's interval after the frame that showed something (`TestEmptyFramesInARowBackOffToPisInterval`, `TestWaitAfterAnEmptyFrameEndsWithPisIntervalAfterTheShownFrame`); a change 1 to 50 ms after a frame is shown no later than under Pi's interval and no sooner than the floor (`TestChangeAfterEmptyFramesIsShownNoLaterThanUnderPisInterval`, `TestEmptyImmediateFrameKeepsTheFloorBetweenShownFrames`); 65 frames in a second that changes nothing (`TestStreamThatChangesNothingIsDrawnAsOftenAsUnderPisInterval`); forced and key-press frames at once after empty frames; an empty frame under Pi's interval restarts Pi's interval (`TestEmptyFrameUnderPisIntervalRestartsPisInterval`); the clamp at the floor, at four times the cost and at Pi's constant; Pi's constant under each failed condition alone, at the next request; forced and key-press frames never delayed, also in place of a pending paced frame (`TestKeyPressAndForcedFrameReplaceAPendingPacedFrameAtOnce`); the last frame of a run drawn after the gate closes (`TestLastFrameOfARunIsDrawnAfterTheGateCloses`); a paced frame shows the newest state (`TestPacedFrameShowsTheNewestState`); a slow forced or key-press frame delays the next frame no longer than Pi's interval, and only that one (`TestSlowImmediateFrameDelaysTheNextFrameNoLongerThanPisInterval`); a blocked write returns the interval to Pi's; one timer a frame; `TestSynchronizedUpdatesTrustedIn` and `TestSynchronizedUpdatesTrustedFromTheImplementingRelease` over the environment and version matrix. `tui/frame_pacing_replay_test.go` (`TestAdaptivePacingShowsTheSameSettledScreens`): the active policy outside a multiplexer, the nine scenarios of the terminal-engine corpus replayed as streams through a fullscreen renderer on a pseudo-terminal with the screen writer on and a listed terminal declared, under Pi's interval and under the policy: 9 streams, 865 steps, 34 settle points; with the policy 228 frames began sooner than Pi's interval allows (224 under the race detector), with Pi's interval none; 0 settled screens differ between the two on any of gridterm's four width models, and the cells of both equal the corpus's screens drawn frame by frame (container job t9, commit 5a1fe04a6). `tui/frame_pacing_allocs_norace_test.go`: the interval allocates nothing. `internal/codingagent/write_call_unchanged_test.go`: `TestWriteCardSkippedDrawsShowTheSameLines` (for random source in random deltas, cut also right before, at and after a new line, a write card drawn only on a change shows after every delta the lines of a card drawn after every delta; an expanded card and a path without a language never skip) and `TestToolCallDeltaAsksForNoFrameWhenTheCardShowsNoChange` (through the handler). `internal/codingagent/frame_pacing_test.go`: `TestARunThatSelectsNoFramePacingHasPisInterval` (the default is `pi`: settings without the key read `pi` and the renderer is given no policy; the setting and the variable each give it one), `TestFramePacingSelectionUnderEitherDefault` (the selection under both values of the default constant), the selection by setting and environment and that the renderer is given the policy only then; the gate closes one frame after a run ends by a drained queue, an error or an abort, during a compaction, at the mount of each extension surface and for each kind of extension-drawn transcript component, and opens again one frame after the unmount or after the transcript is emptied; `TestTerminalFramePacingSetting`: global file only, normalized, kept by a rewrite. The startup warning: `TestAdaptiveFramePacingLacksScreenWriter` (the decision, over both settings and both variables) and `TestStartupNoticeWhenAdaptivePacingLacksTheScreenWriter` (an interactive start from a settings file: the warning once in the transcript and on the screen; none with both selected, only the writer, or neither) in `internal/codingagent/frame_pacing_test.go`; `TestScreenWriterSelected` (`tui/terminal_screen_writer_switch_test.go`); `TestFramePacingNoticeStaysOutOfPrintJSONAndRPC` (`coding/cli/frame_pacing_notice_modes_test.go`): the stdout and stderr of print, JSON and RPC mode are those of a run that selects nothing.

Parity allowance: none for a run that selects nothing: it gives the renderer no policy and shows no warning, and in a tmux pane, which is where the parity runner drives PiG, a selected policy never answers, so no scenario sees a paced frame under either value. One allowance, for a suite run that selects the policy without the screen writer (`PIG_FRAME_PACING=adaptive`; the parity gates select neither): PiG's transcript then holds the startup warning, `Warning: Adaptive frame pacing (terminal.framePacing, PIG_FRAME_PACING) has no effect without the screen writer (terminal.screenWriter "writer"): frames keep Pi's 16 ms interval.`, where Pi's holds nothing, and three scenarios fail, because their asserted crop includes rows where that block is drawn: `interactive-rendering/22-exit-final-layout` and `fullscreen/09-main-screen-compaction-in-progress` (the main screen of a resumed Session: the block stands between the resumed transcript and what the scenario adds) and `fullscreen/13-alt-screen-search-input` (the search box overlays the two startup rows the block occupies). The scenarios, their comparators and their crops are unchanged, and they pass with nothing selected. Kept check: `TestFramePacingNoticeIsTheOnlyDifferenceUnderAdaptivePacing` (`test/parity/runner/frame_pacing_notice_allowance_test.go`) runs each of the three with `PIG_FRAME_PACING=adaptive` and `PIG_TUI_SCREEN_WRITER=pi` added to PiG's environment, requires the scenario's own assertions to fail, cuts the block from PiG's capture, and requires the scenario's own assertions to pass on what is left: PiG's capture without the block is Pi's. It fails when another glyph differs (it changes one itself and requires the assertions to fail again), when the block is not found exactly once, and when this record does not state the warning's text or name the scenario. It is part of every parity run that runs the scenario (`make parity-fast`, `make parity-family FAMILY=fullscreen`, `make parity-family FAMILY=interactive-rendering`), whatever the two variables hold. The cell `PIG_FRAME_PACING=adaptive` with the screen writer not selected, as run on 2026-11-13 (`make parity-family`, the hermetic scenarios of the five families this record's lane runs; no other family was run in that cell):

| family | scenarios run | passed | allowed: the warning's rows | other failures |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| tools | 34 | 34 | 0 | 0 |
| interactive-rendering | 80 | 79 | 1 (`22-exit-final-layout`) | 0 |
| fullscreen | 19 | 17 | 2 (`09-main-screen-compaction-in-progress`, `13-alt-screen-search-input`) | 0 |
| tui-components | 40 | 40 | 0 | 0 |
| oauth | 32 | 32 | 0 | 0 |

With nothing selected, with only the screen writer, and with both, the five families pass whole.

Remove when: Pi adopts an adaptive scheduler of its own, or the owner declines the policy. Then the policy, `synchronizedUpdatesTrustedIn`, the setting, the variable, the markers and this record go; the hook returns to Pi's constant.

Approval: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-11-12 for 0.8.0 (14:42Z, with conditions; the conditions as recorded here at 15:47Z): a positive list of terminals known to implement synchronized updates; no multiplexers; an unknown terminal gets Pi's interval; active only in fullscreen mode while a reply streams and no compaction runs; never with extension UI mounted; default `adaptive`, the other value `pi`; never enable pacing for unknown terminals to win a benchmark row. Where each is enforced: the list, the multiplexers and the unknown terminal in `synchronizedUpdatesTrustedIn` (`tui/tui_alt_screen_scroll.go`); fullscreen in `frameIntervalLocked` (`tui/frame_pacing.go`), which asks the terminal's `ScreenWriterActive`; streaming, compaction and extension UI in `replyStreamsUndisturbed` and `extensionDrawsOnScreen` (`internal/codingagent/frame_pacing.go`), stored before every throttled frame by `allowFramePacing`; the setting in `installFramePacing`. Shipped default: `pi`, not the `adaptive` of the approval's list, by the release ruling of 2026-11-12 on the measurement above; `adaptive` stays selectable. Owner decision recorded 2026-11-12T23:22:43Z (owner: "proceed with recommendations"): "Adaptive pacing: form B ships OPT-IN; default stays 'pi'; D625 records the numbers." Form B is the rule for frames that send nothing together with the handler's skip of updates that change nothing, the form measured above.
By owner principle (decision 29, 2026-11-17), checked by Claude and Tern
